Lemma 3.16
.

For every
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
the maps

\Phi\mapsto\left[\Phi,\nu\right]_{r}

and

(\Phi,\Psi)\mapsto\left[\Phi,\Psi\right]_{r}

(resp.
\Phi\mapsto\left[\Phi,\nu\right]_{l}

and
(\Phi,\Psi)\mapsto\left[\Phi,\Psi\right]_{l}
)
are convex (resp. concave) in

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu)
and

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu)\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\nu)
.

Proof.

We prove the convexity of

(\Phi,\Psi)\mapsto\left[\Phi,\Psi\right]_{r}
in

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu)\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\nu)
;
the argument of the proofs of
the other statements are completely analogous.

Let
\Phi_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu)
,

\Psi_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\nu)
,
and let
\beta_{k}\geq 0
,
with
\sum_{k}\beta_{k}=1
,
k=1,\cdots,K
.
We set
\Phi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}\Phi_{k}
,

\Psi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}\Psi_{k}
,
For every
k
let us select

\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{k}\in\Lambda(\Phi_{k},\Psi_{k})
such that

\left[\Phi_{k},\Psi_{k}\right]_{r}=\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{k}.

It is not difficult to check that

\boldsymbol{\Theta}:=\sum_{k}\beta_{k}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{k}\in\Lambda(\Phi,\Psi)

so that

\left[\Phi,\Psi\right]_{r}\leq\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\sum_{k}\beta_{k}\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{k}=\sum_{k}\beta_{k}\left[\Phi_{k},\Psi_{k}\right]_{r}.\qed

3.4.
Behaviour of duality pairings along geodesics

We have seen that
the duality pairings
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{r}
and
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{l}

may differ when the collection of optimal plans

\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
contains more than one element.
It is natural to expect a simpler behaviour along geodesics.
We will introduce the following
definition, where we use the notation

\mathsf{x}^{t}(x_{0},x_{1}):=(1-t)x_{0}+tx_{1},\quad\mathsf{v}^{0}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1}):=v_{0}\quad\text{for every }(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1})\in\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X},\,t\in[0,1].

Definition 3.17
.

For
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
,
t\in[0,1]
,

\vartheta_{t}=\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
and

\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\vartheta_{t})
,
we set

\Gamma_{t}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}):=\left\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X})\mid(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\vartheta},\quad(\mathsf{x}^{t}\circ(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1}),\mathsf{v}^{0})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\Phi\right\},

which is not empty since
\vartheta_{t}=\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi
.
We set

\displaystyle\left[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right]_{b,t}

\displaystyle:=\int\langle x_{0}-x_{1},\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(\mathsf{x}^{t}(x_{0},x_{1}))\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},x_{1}),

\displaystyle[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,t}

\displaystyle:=\min\left\{\int\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1})\mid\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Gamma_{t}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\right\},

\displaystyle[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,t}

\displaystyle:=\max\left\{\int\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1})\mid\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Gamma_{t}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\right\}.

If moreover
\Phi_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\vartheta_{0})
,

\Phi_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\vartheta_{1})
,
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma(\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1})
, we define

\displaystyle[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}]_{r,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}

\displaystyle:=[\Phi_{0},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,0}-[\Phi_{1},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,1},

\displaystyle[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}]_{l,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}

\displaystyle:=[\Phi_{0},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,0}-[\Phi_{1},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,1}.

Notice that, if
\Phi_{x}
is the disintegration of
\Phi
with respect to

\vartheta_{t}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi
, we can consider
the barycentric coupling
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{t}:=\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\Phi_{\mathsf{x}^{t}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma_{t}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
, i.e.

\int\psi(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{t}=\int\Big[\int\psi(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{(1-t)x_{0}+tx_{1}}(v_{0})\Big]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},x_{1})

so that

\left[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right]_{b,t}=\int\langle{v_{0}},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{t}
and

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,t}\leq\left[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right]_{b,t}\leq[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,t}.

If we define by
\mathsf{s}:\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathsf{X}^{2}
the map
\mathsf{s}(x_{0},x_{1}):=(x_{1},x_{0})

(with a similar definition for
\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X}
:
\mathsf{s}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1}):=(x_{1},v_{0},x_{0})
) it is easy to check that

\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Gamma_{t}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Gamma_{1-t}(\Phi,\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

so that

(3.24)

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,t}=-[\Phi,\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,1-t},\quad[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,t}=-[\Phi,\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,1-t}.

(
3.15
) and (
3.18
) have simpler versions in two
particular cases, which will be explained in the next remark.

Remark 3.18

(Particular cases)
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2})
,
t\in[0,1]
,
\vartheta_{t}=\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
,

\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\vartheta_{t})
and
\mathsf{x}^{t}:\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathsf{X}
is

\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
-essentially injective so that

\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
is concentrated on a Borel map
(X_{0},X_{1}):\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}
,
i.e.
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=(X_{0},X_{1})_{\sharp}\vartheta_{t}
.
In this case
\Gamma_{t}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

contains a unique element
given by

(X_{0}\circ\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v},X_{1}\circ\mathsf{x})_{\sharp}\Phi

and

(3.25)

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,t}=[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,t}=\left[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right]_{b,t}=\int\langle v,X_{0}(x)-X_{1}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi(x,v)=\int\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi},X_{0}-X_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{t},

where in the last formula we have applied
the barycentric reduction (
3.8
).
When
t=0
and
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
is the unique element of

\Gamma_{o}(\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1})
then

X_{0}(x)=x
and
we obtain

\left[\Phi,\vartheta_{1}\right]_{r}=\left[\Phi,\vartheta_{1}\right]_{l}=[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,0}=[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,0}=\int\langle v,x-X_{1}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi(x,v)=\int\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi},x-X_{1}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{0}(x).

Another simple case is when
\Phi=\Phi_{\vartheta_{t},\boldsymbol{w}}
for some
vector field
\boldsymbol{w}\in L^{2}_{\vartheta_{t}}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})
as in (
3.5
)
(i.e. its disintegration
\Phi_{x}
w.r.t.
\vartheta_{t}
takes the form

\delta_{\boldsymbol{w}(x)}
and
\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}.
).
We have

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,t}=[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,t}=\int\langle\boldsymbol{w}((1-t)x_{0}+tx_{1}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},x_{1}).

In particular we get

\left[\Phi,\vartheta_{1}\right]_{r}=\min\Big\{\int\langle\boldsymbol{w}(x),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},x_{1})\mid\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma_{o}(\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1})\Big\}.

An important case in which
the previous Remark
3.18
applies
is that of geodesics in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
,
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be a
constant speed geodesic induced by an optimal plan
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
by the relation

\mu_{t}=\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu},\quad t\in[0,1],\quad\mathsf{x}^{t}(x_{0},x_{1})=(1-t)x_{0}+tx_{1}.

If
t\in(0,1)
,
\Phi_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu_{t})
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{0})
, then

(3.26)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-t}\left[\Phi_{t},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-t}\left[\Phi_{t},\mu_{1}\right]_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\Phi_{t},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\Phi_{t},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{t}\left[\Phi_{t},\mu_{0}\right]_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{t}\left[\Phi_{t},\mu_{0}\right]_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-[\Phi_{t},\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]_{r,1-t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-[\Phi_{t},\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]_{l,1-t}.

Proof.

The crucial fact is that
\mathsf{x}^{t}:\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathsf{X}

is
injective on
\supp(\boldsymbol{\mu})
and thus a bijection on its image
\supp(\mu_{t})
. Indeed, take
(x_{0},x_{1}),(x_{0}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime})\in\supp(\boldsymbol{\mu})
, then

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{x}^{t}(x_{0},x_{1})-\mathsf{x}^{t}(x_{0}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-t)^{2}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|^{2}+t^{2}|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|^{2}+2t(1-t)\langle x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime},x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq(1-t)^{2}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|^{2}+t^{2}|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|^{2}

thanks to the cyclical monotonicity of
\supp(\boldsymbol{\mu})

(see
[
AGS08
, Remark 7.1.2]
).

Then, for every
x\in\supp(\mu_{t})
, there exists a unique couple

(x_{0},x_{1})=(X_{0}(x),X_{1}(x))\in\supp(\boldsymbol{\mu})
s.t.
x=(1-t)x_{0}+tx_{1}
, where we refer to Remark
3.18
for the definitions of
X_{0},X_{1}

(cf. also
[
San15
, Theorem 5.29]
).
Hence, in the following diagram all maps are bijections:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supp(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supp(\boldsymbol{\mu})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supp(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t1})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supp(\mu_{t})}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{x}^{t}\text{,}\,\mathsf{x}^{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{x}^{t}\text{,}\,\mathsf{x}^{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{x}^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}}\text{,}\,X_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}}\text{,}\,X_{0})}

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t1}=(\mathsf{x}^{t},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},X_{1})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}
is the unique
element of
\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t},\mu_{1})
and

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t0}=(\mathsf{x}^{t},\mathsf{x}^{0})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},X_{0})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}=(\mathsf{x}^{1-t},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is the unique element of

\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t},\mu_{0})

(see Theorem
2.8
).
Since

\frac{x-X_{1}(x)}{1-t}=\frac{x-x_{1}}{1-t}=x_{0}-x_{1}=-\frac{x-x_{0}}{t}=-\frac{x-X_{0}(x)}{t},

and
\Lambda(\Phi_{t},\mu_{1})=\{(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}},X_{1}\circ\mathsf{x})_{\sharp}\Phi_{t}\}

thanks to Theorem
2.8
, by Theorem

3.9
and Corollary
3.10
we have

\left[\Phi_{t},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}=\left[\Phi_{t},\mu_{1}\right]_{l}=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle v,x-X_{1}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}(x,v).

Analogously,
\Lambda(\Phi_{t},\mu_{0})=\{(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}},X_{0}\circ\mathsf{x})_{\sharp}\Phi_{t}\}
. Hence

\left[\Phi_{t},\mu_{0}\right]_{r}=\left[\Phi_{t},\mu_{0}\right]_{l}=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle v,x-X_{0}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}(x,v).

Also recalling (
3.24
) and (
3.25
) we conclude.
∎

4.
Dissipative probability vector fields: the metric viewpoint

4.1.
Multivalued Probability Vector Fields and
\lambda
-dissipativity

Definition 4.1

(Multivalued Probability Vector Field - MPVF)
.

A
multivalued probability vector field

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a nonempty subset of

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
with domain
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}):=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\{\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi:\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\}
.
Given
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, we define the
section

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]

of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
as

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:=(\mathsf{x}_{\sharp})^{-1}(\mu)\cap{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}=\left\{\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\mid\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi=\mu\right\}.

A
selection

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a subset of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
such that
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime})=\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.
We call
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
a
probability vector field
(PVF) if
\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}
is injective in
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
, i.e.
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
contains a
unique element for every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a vector field if for
every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
contains
a unique element
\Phi
concentrated on a map,
i.e.
\Phi=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi})_{\sharp}\mu
.

Remark 4.2
.

We can equivalently formulate Definition

4.1

by considering
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
as a multifunction,
as in the case, e.g., of the Wasserstein subdifferential

\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}
of a function
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
, see

[
AGS08
, Ch. 10]
and the next Section
5.1
.
According to this viewpoint, a MPVF is a set-valued map
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\supset\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
such that
\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi=\mu
for all
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
.
In this way, each section
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
is nothing but the image of
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
through
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.
In this case,
probability vector fields
correspond to single
valued maps: this notion has been used in

[
Pic19
]

with the aim of describing a sort of velocity field on
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})
, and later in
[
Pic18
]
dealing with Multivalued Probability Vector Fields (called Probability Multifunctions).

Definition 4.3

(Metrically
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF)
.

A MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is (metrically)
\lambda
-dissipative
,

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, if

(4.1)

\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\quad\text{for every }\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\ \mu_{i}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{i}.

We say that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is (metrically)
\lambda
-accretive
, if
-{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}=\{-\Phi:\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\}

(recall (
3.14
)) is
-\lambda
-dissipative, i.e.

\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{l}\geq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\quad\text{for every }\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\ \mu_{i}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{i}.

Remark 4.4
.

Notice that (
4.1
) is equivalent to ask for the existence of
a coupling
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})

(thus
(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\Theta}
is optimal
between
\mu_{0}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{0}
and
\mu_{1}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{1}
)
such that

\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\lambda\int|x_{1}-x_{0}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}.

Recalling the discussion of the previous section,

\lambda
-dissipativity has a natural metric interpretation:
for every
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with
\mu_{0}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{0}
,

\mu_{1}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{1}

we have
the asymptotic expansion

W_{2}^{2}(\operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{t}\Phi_{0},\operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{t}\Phi_{1})\leq(1+2\lambda t)W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+o(t)\quad\text{as }t\downarrow 0.

Remark 4.5
.

Thanks to Corollary

3.7
, (
4.1
) implies the weaker condition

(4.2)

\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}+\left[\Phi_{1},\mu_{0}\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}),\quad\forall\,\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\ \mu_{0}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{0},\ \mu_{1}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{1}.

It is clear that the inequality of (
4.2
) implies
the inequality of (
4.1
) whenever
\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

contains only one element. More generally,
we will
see in Corollary
4.13
that (
4.2
) is in fact equivalent to (
4.1
)
when
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is geodesically
convex (according to Definition
2.7
).

As in the standard Hilbert case,
\lambda
-dissipativity can be
reduced to dissipativity (meaning
0
-dissipativity) by a simple transformation. Let us introduce
the map

L^{\lambda}:\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\to\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX},\quad L^{\lambda}(x,v):=(x,v-\lambda x),

observing that for every
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X})

with
(\mathsf{x}^{i})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\mu_{i}
,
i=0,1
, the
transformed plan
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\lambda}:=(L^{\lambda},\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfies

\displaystyle\int\langle v_{0},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\int\langle v_{0}-\lambda x_{0},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}

(4.3)

\displaystyle=\int\langle v_{0},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\Big(\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1})-\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0})\Big).

Similarly, if
\Theta\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

with
\mathsf{x}^{i}_{\sharp}\Theta=\mu_{i}
,
the plan
\Theta^{\lambda}:=(L^{\lambda},L^{\lambda})_{\sharp}\Theta

satisfies

\displaystyle\int\langle v_{0}-v_{1},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Theta^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\int\langle v_{0}-v_{1}-\lambda(x_{0}-x_{1}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Theta

(4.4)

\displaystyle=\int\langle v_{0}-v_{1},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Theta-\lambda\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Theta.

Lemma 4.6
.

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF
(resp. satisfies (
4.2
)) if and only if

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\lambda}:=L^{\lambda}_{\sharp}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\{L^{\lambda}_{\sharp}\Phi\mid\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\}

is dissipative (resp. satisfies (
4.2
) with
\lambda=0
).

Proof.

Let us first check the case of (
4.2
).
Since
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Lambda_{o}(\Phi_{0},\mu_{1})
if and only
if

\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{o}(L^{\lambda}_{\sharp}\Phi_{0},\mu_{1})
,
(
4.3
) yields

\displaystyle\int\langle v_{0},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\lambda}=\int\langle v_{0},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\Big(\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0})-\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1})+W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\Big)

and therefore

(4.5)

\left[L^{\lambda}_{\sharp}\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}=\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\Big(\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0})-\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1})+W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\Big).

Using the corresponding identity for
\left[L^{\lambda}_{\sharp}\Phi_{1},\mu_{0}\right]_{r}
we obtain that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\lambda}
is dissipative.

A similar argument, using the identity (
4.4
), shows
the equivalence between the
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and
the dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\lambda}
.
∎

Let us conclude this section by showing that
\lambda
-dissipativity can be deduced from a Lipschitz like condition similar to the one considered in
[
Pic19
]
(see Appendix
A
).

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose that the
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
satisfies

\mathcal{W}_{2}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu],{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu^{\prime}])\leq LW_{2}(\nu,\nu^{\prime}),\quad\forall\,\nu,\nu^{\prime}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),

where

\mathcal{W}_{2}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})\to[0,+\infty)
is
defined by

\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}|v_{0}-v_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1},v_{1}):\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\right\},

with
\Lambda(\cdot,\cdot)
as in Definition
3.8
.
Then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative, for
\lambda:=\frac{1}{2}(1+L^{2})

Proof.

Let
\nu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, then by Theorem
3.9
and Young’s inequality, we have

\begin{split}\left[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu^{\prime}],{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu^{\prime\prime}]\right]_{r}&=\min\left\{\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle x^{\prime}-x^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime}-v^{\prime\prime}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\,:\,\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Lambda({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu^{\prime}],{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu^{\prime\prime}])\right\}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(W_{2}^{2}(\nu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime})+\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu^{\prime}],{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu^{\prime\prime}])\right)\\
&\leq\frac{L^{2}+1}{2}\,W_{2}^{2}(\nu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime}).\qed\end{split}

4.2.
Behaviour of
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF along geodesics

Let us now study the behaviour of a MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
along geodesics.
Recall that in the case of a dissipative map
{\boldsymbol{F}}:\mathsf{H}\to\mathsf{H}
in a Hilbert
space
\mathsf{H}
, it is quite immediate to prove that the real function

f(t):=\langle F(x_{t}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle,\quad x_{t}=(1-t)x_{0}+tx_{1},\quad t\in[0,1]

is monotone increasing.
This property has a natural counterpart
in the case of measures.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
,
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
. We define the sets

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}):={}

\displaystyle\Big\{t\in[0,1]:\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\Big\},

(4.6)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{o}^{i}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}):={}

\displaystyle\Big\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}):i\text{ is an accumulation point of }\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\Big\},i=0,1

\displaystyle\Gamma_{o}^{01}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}):={}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\cap\Gamma_{o}^{1}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}).

Notice that these sets depend on
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
just through
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
. In
particular, if
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is open or
geodesically convex according to
Definition
2.7
then

\Gamma_{o}^{01}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\neq\emptyset
.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a
MPVF.
Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

and let
\mu_{t}:=\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
t\in[0,1]
.
For every
t\in\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
we define

\displaystyle[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}:=\sup\left\{[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}\mid\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]\right\},\qquad[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}:=\inf\left\{[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}\mid\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]\right\}.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let us suppose that the
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

satisfies (
4.2
),
let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
, and let

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
with

W^{2}:=W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
. Then the following properties hold

(1)

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}
for every

t\in(0,1)\cap\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
;

(2)

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}+\lambda W^{2}(t-s)

for every
s,t\in\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
s<t
;

(3)

t\mapsto[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}+\lambda W^{2}t
and

t\mapsto[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}+\lambda W^{2}t
are increasing respectively in
\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\setminus\{1\}

and in
\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\setminus\{0\}
.

(4)

the right (resp. left)
limits
of
t\mapsto[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}
and
t\mapsto[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}
exist at every right (resp. left) accumulation point of

\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
and in those points the right (resp. left) limits of

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}

coincide with the right (resp. left) limits of
[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}
.

(5)

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}=[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}
at every
interior point

t
of
\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

where one of them is continuous.

Proof.

Throughout all the proof we set
f_{r}(t):=[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}
and
f_{l}(t):=[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}
.
Thanks to Lemma
4.6
and in particular to (
4.5
), it is easy to check that
it is sufficient to consider the dissipative case
\lambda=0
.

(1)

It is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.19
and the definitions of
f_{r}
and
f_{l}
.

(2)

We prove that for every
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{s}]
and
\Phi^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]
it holds

(4.7)

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}\leq[\Phi^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}.

The thesis will follow immediately passing to the
\sup
over
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{s}]
in the LHS and to the
\inf
over
\Phi^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]
in the RHS. It is enough to prove (
4.7
) in case at least one between
s,t
belongs to
(0,1)
. Let us define the map
L:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X})\to\mathbb{R}
as

L(\gamma):=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle v_{0},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\gamma(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1})\quad\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X}).

Observe that, since it never happens that
s=0
and
t=1
at the same time, the map
T:\Gamma_{s}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu})\to\Lambda(\Phi,\mu_{t})
defined as

T(\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=(\mathsf{x}^{s}\circ(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1}),\mathsf{v}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{t}\circ(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1}))_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}

is a bijection s.t.
(t-s)L(\boldsymbol{\sigma})=L(T(\boldsymbol{\sigma}))
for every
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Gamma_{s}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu})
. This immediately gives that

(t-s)[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}=\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}.

In the same way we can deduce that

(s-t)[\Phi^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}=\left[\Phi^{\prime},\mu_{s}\right]_{r}.

Thanks to the dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
we get

(t-s)[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}-(t-s)[\Phi^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}=\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}+\left[\Phi^{\prime},\mu_{s}\right]_{r}\leq 0.

(3)

Combining (1) and (2) we have that for every
s,t\in\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
with
0<s<t<1
it holds

(4.8)

f_{l}(s)\leq f_{r}(s)\leq f_{l}(t)\leq f_{r}(t).

This implies that both
f_{l}
and
f_{r}
are increasing in
\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\cap(0,1)
. Observe that, again combining (1) and (2), it also holds

\displaystyle f_{r}(0)

\displaystyle\leq f_{l}(t)\leq f_{r}(t),

\displaystyle f_{l}(t)

\displaystyle\leq f_{r}(t)\leq f_{l}(1)

for every
t\in\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\setminus\{0,1\}
, and then
f_{r}
is increasing in

\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\setminus\{1\}
and
f_{l}
is increasing in
\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\setminus\{0\}
.

(4)

It is an immediate consequence of (
4.8
).

(5)

It is a straightforward consequence of (4).
∎

Thanks to the previous Theorem

4.10
the next definition is well posed.

Definition 4.11
.

Let us suppose that the MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

satisfies (
4.2
),
let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
.

\displaystyle\text{If
$\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})$ we set}\quad[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}

\displaystyle:=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}

\displaystyle\text{If
$\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{1}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})$ we set}\quad[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}

\displaystyle:=\lim_{t\uparrow 1}[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}=\lim_{t\uparrow 1}[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}.

Corollary 4.12
.

Let us keep the same notation of Theorem

4.10

and let
s\in\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\cap(0,1)

with
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{s}]
.

(1)

If
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
we have that

(4.9)

\displaystyle[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}

\displaystyle\leq[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,s}+\lambda sW^{2}=[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}+\lambda sW^{2};

if moreover

\Phi_{0}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{0}]
then

(4.10)

\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}\leq[\Phi_{0},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}.

(2)

If
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{1}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
we have that

\displaystyle[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,s}-\lambda(1-s)W^{2}=[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}-\lambda(1-s)W^{2}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-};

if moreover
\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{1}]
then

(4.11)

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}\leq[\Phi_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,1}\leq-\left[\Phi_{1},\mu_{0}\right]_{r}

(3)

In particular, for every
\Phi_{0}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{0}]
,
\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{1}]
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{01}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

we obtain

(4.12)

[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}]_{r,\boldsymbol{\mu}}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}-[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}\leq\lambda W^{2}_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

(
4.12
) immediately yields the following property.

Corollary 4.13
.

Suppose that a
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
satisfies

(4.13)

for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

the set
\Gamma_{o}^{01}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
of (
4.6
) is not empty

(e.g. if
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is open or geodesically convex),
then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative if and only if it satisfies
(
4.2
).

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a
MPVF
satisfying (
4.2
),
let
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

and
let
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu_{0})
.
Consider the following statements

(\mathrm{P1})

\left[\Phi,\mu\right]_{r}+\left[\Psi,\mu_{0}\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu)

for every
\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with
\mu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Psi
;

(\mathrm{P2})

for every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
there exists
\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
s.t.
\left[\Phi,\mu\right]_{r}+\left[\Psi,\mu_{0}\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu)
;

(\mathrm{P3})

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}
for every

\mu_{1}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
;

(\mathrm{P4})

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}
for every

\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
;

(\mathrm{P5})

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}

for every

\mu_{1}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{1}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
;

(\mathrm{P6})

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}

for every

\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{1}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.

Then the following hold

(1)

(
P1
)

\Rightarrow

(
P2
)

\Rightarrow

(
P3
)

\Rightarrow

(
P4
)

;

(2)

(
P1
)

\Rightarrow

(
P2
)

\Rightarrow

(
P5
)

\Rightarrow

(
P6
)

;

(3)

if for every
\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\neq\emptyset
, then

(
P4
)

\Rightarrow

(
P1
)

(in particular,

(
P1
)

,

(
P2
)

,

(
P3
)

,

(
P4
)

are equivalent);

(4)

if
for every
\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

\Gamma_{o}^{1}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\neq\emptyset
, then

(
P6
)

\Rightarrow

(
P1
)

(in particular,

(
P1
)

,

(
P2
)

,

(
P5
)

,

(
P6
)

are equivalent).

Proof.

We first prove that

(
P2
)

\Rightarrow

(
P3
)

,

(
P5
)

.
Let us choose an arbitrary
\mu_{1}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
;
by the definition of
[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}
and arguing as in the proof of Theorem
4.10
(2),
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
and
t\in\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

there exists
\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]
such that

\displaystyle[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}\leq-\frac{1}{t}\left[\Psi,\mu_{0}\right]_{r}+t\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=[\Psi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}+t\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

\displaystyle\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}+t\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

where we also used (
3.26
).
If
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
by passing to the limit as
t\downarrow 0
we get

(
P3
)

.

In the second case, assuming that
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{1}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
we can pass to the limit as
t\uparrow 1
and we get

(
P5
)

.

We now prove item (
3
). Let
\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{1}]
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
s\in\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\cap(0,1)
,
\Phi_{s}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{s}]
, with
\mu_{s}=\mathsf{x}^{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
. Assuming

(
P4
)

and using (
4.10
), (
4.9
), (
3.26
) and (
4.2
), we have

\displaystyle\left[\Phi,\mu_{1}\right]_{r}

\displaystyle\leq[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}\leq[\Phi_{s},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}+\lambda sW_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-s}\left[\Phi_{s},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}+\lambda sW_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\leq-\frac{1}{1-s}\left[\Psi,\mu_{s}\right]_{r}+\lambda(1+s)W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

By Lemma
3.14
, letting
s\downarrow 0
we get

(
P1
)

.
Item (
4
) follows by (
4.10
), (
4.11
).
∎

4.3.
Extensions of dissipative MPVF

Let us briefly study a few simple properties about
extensions of
\lambda
-dissipative MPVFs.
The first one concerns the sequential closure in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

(the sequential closure may be smaller than the topological closure,
but see Proposition
2.15
):
given
A\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
, we will denote
by
\operatorname{cl}(A)
its sequential closure defined by

\operatorname{cl}(A):=\Big\{\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}):\exists\,\Phi_{n}\in A:\Phi_{n}\to\Phi\ \text{in }\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})\Big\}.

Proposition 4.15
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
then
its sequential closure

\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

is
\lambda
-dissipative as well.

Proof.

If
\Phi^{i}
,
i=0,1
, belong to
\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
we can find sequences
\Phi^{i}_{n}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
such that

\Phi^{i}_{n}\to\Phi^{i}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

as
n\to\infty
,
i=0,1
. It is then sufficient to pass to the limit in the inequality

\left[\Phi^{0}_{n},\Phi^{1}_{n}\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{0}_{n},\mu^{1}_{n}),\quad\mu^{i}_{n}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi^{i}_{n}

using the lower semicontinuity property (
3.22
)
and the fact that convergence in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
yields

\mu^{i}_{n}\to\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi^{i}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
as
n\to\infty
.
∎

A second result concerns the convexification of the sections of

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.
For every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
we set

\displaystyle\operatorname{co}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})[\mu]:={}

\displaystyle\text{the convex hull of }{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]=\Big\{\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}\Phi_{k}:\Phi_{k}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu],\alpha_{k}\geq 0,\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}=1\Big\},

\displaystyle\overline{\operatorname{co}}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})[\mu]:={}

\displaystyle\operatorname{cl}(\operatorname{co}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})[\mu]).

Notice that if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
is bounded in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
then

\overline{\operatorname{co}}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})[\mu]
coincides with the closed convex hull of

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
.

Proposition 4.16
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative, then

\operatorname{co}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and
\overline{\operatorname{co}}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
are
\lambda
-dissipative as well.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.15
and noting that
\overline{\operatorname{co}}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\subset\operatorname{cl}(\operatorname{co}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}))
,
it is sufficient to prove that
\operatorname{co}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is
\lambda
-dissipative.
By Lemma
4.6
it is not restrictive to assume
\lambda=0
.
Let
\Phi^{i}\in\operatorname{co}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})[\mu_{i}]
,
i=0,1
;
there exist positive coefficients
\alpha^{i}_{k}
,
k=1,\cdots,K
,
with
\sum_{k}\alpha^{i}_{k}=1
,
and elements
\Phi^{i}_{k}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu^{i}]
,
i=0,1
,
such that
\Phi^{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\alpha^{i}_{k}\Phi^{i}_{k}
.
Setting
\beta_{h,k}:=\alpha^{0}_{h}\alpha^{1}_{k}
, we can apply
Lemma
3.16

and we obtain

\left[\Phi^{0},\Phi^{1}\right]_{r}=\Big[{\sum_{h,k}\beta_{h,k}\Phi^{0}_{h}},{\sum_{h,k}\beta_{h,k}\Phi^{1}_{k}}\Big]_{r}\leq\sum_{h,k}\beta_{h,k}\left[\Phi^{0}_{h},\Phi^{1}_{k}\right]_{r}\leq 0.\qed

As a last step, we want to study the
properties of the extended MPVF

(4.14)

\displaystyle\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}:=\Big\{

\displaystyle\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}):\mu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})},

\displaystyle\left[\Phi,\nu\right]_{r}+\left[\Psi,\mu\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\quad\forall\,\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\ \nu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Psi\Big\}.

It is obvious that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
; if the domain of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
satisfies the geometric condition
(
4.16
), the following result shows that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
provides
the maximal
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
.

(a)

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}\supset{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative
with
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime})\subset\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
, then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
. In particular
\overline{\operatorname{co}}(\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}))\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

(b)

\widehat{{\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and

\widehat{{\operatorname{co}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

(c)

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is sequentially closed and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
is
convex
for every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.

(d)

If
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
satisfies (
4.13
), then
the restriction of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
to
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is

\lambda
-dissipative and
for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

(4.15)

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}=[\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+},\quad[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}=[\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}\quad\text{for every }\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{01}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}).

(e)

If
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})},\ \mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

and
\Gamma_{o}^{1}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\neq\emptyset

then

\Phi_{i}\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{i}]\quad\Rightarrow\quad\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

(f)

If

(4.16)

for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

the set
\Gamma_{o}^{01}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is not
empty,

then
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative as well
and for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

(
4.15
) holds.

Proof.

Claim (a) is obvious since every
\lambda
-dissipative extension
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}

of

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
in
\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
satisfies
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

(b) Let us prove that if
\Phi\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

then

\Phi\in\widehat{\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
. If
\Psi\in\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
we
can find a sequence
\Psi_{n}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
converging to

\Psi
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
as
n\to\infty
.
We can then pass to the limit in the inequalities

\left[\Phi,\nu_{n}\right]_{r}+\left[\Phi_{n},\mu\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu_{n}),\quad\mu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi,\ \nu_{n}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Psi_{n},

using the lower semicontinuity results of Lemma
3.14
. We conclude since
\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}=\overline{\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}))}
.

In order to prove that
\Phi\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\ \Rightarrow\ \Phi\in\widehat{{\operatorname{co}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}}
we take

\Psi=\sum\alpha_{k}\Psi_{k}\in\operatorname{co}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
;
for some
\Psi_{k}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]
,
\nu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Psi\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, and positive coefficients

\alpha_{k}
,
k=1,\cdots,K
, with
\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}=1
.
Taking a convex combination of the inequalities

\left[\Phi,\nu\right]_{r}+\left[\Psi_{k},\mu\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu),\quad\text{for every }k=1,\cdots,K,

and using Lemma
3.16

we obtain

\left[\Phi,\nu\right]_{r}+\left[\Psi,\mu\right]_{r}\leq\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}\Big(\left[\Phi,\nu\right]_{r}+\left[\Psi_{k},\mu\right]_{r}\Big)\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu).

The proof of claim (c) follows by a similar argument.

(d)
Let
\mu_{i}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,

\Phi_{i}\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{i}]
,
i=0,1
, and

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{01}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.
The implication

(
P1
)

\Rightarrow

(
P4
)

of Proposition
4.14

applied to
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and to
\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}

yields

[\Phi_{0},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+},\quad[\Phi_{1},\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}=-[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}

so that (
4.12
) yields

\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{r}\leq[\Phi_{0},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}+[\Phi_{1},\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}-[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

In order to prove (
4.15
)
we observe that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
so that, for every
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{01}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and every

t\in\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
we have

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}\leq[\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}
and

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}\geq[\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}
,
hence (
4.15
) is a consequence of Definition

4.11
and Theorem
4.10
.

The proof of claim (f) follows by the same argument.

In the case of claim (e),
we use
the implication

(
P1
)

\Rightarrow

(
P6
)

of Proposition
4.14

applied to
\boldsymbol{\mu}

and the implication

(
P1
)

\Rightarrow

(
P3
)

applied to
\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
, obtaining

[\Phi_{0},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-},\quad[\Phi_{1},\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}=-[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}

and then

\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{r}\leq[\Phi_{0},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}+[\Phi_{1},\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).\qed

5.
Examples of
\lambda
-dissipative MPVFs

In this section we present significant examples of
\lambda
-dissipative MPVFs
which are interesting for applications.

5.1.
Subdifferentials of
\lambda
-convex functionals

Recall that
a functional

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
is

\lambda
-(geodesically) convex on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
(see
[
AGS08
, Definition 9.1.1]
)
if
for any
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
in the proper domain

D(\mathcal{F}):=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\mid\mathcal{F}(\mu)<+\infty\}

there exists
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
such that

\mathcal{F}(\mu_{t})\leq(1-t)\mathcal{F}(\mu_{0})+t\mathcal{F}(\mu_{1})-\frac{\lambda}{2}t(1-t)W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],

where
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is the constant speed geodesic induced by
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, i.e.
\mu_{t}=\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
.

The
Fréchet subdifferential
\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}
of
\mathcal{F}

[
AGS08
, Definition 10.3.1]

is a MPVF which can be characterized
[
AGS08
, Theorem 10.3.6]
by

\Phi\in\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}[\mu]\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mu\in D(\mathcal{F}),\ \mathcal{F}(\nu)-\mathcal{F}(\mu)\geq-\left[\Phi,\nu\right]_{l}+\frac{\lambda}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\quad\text{for every }\nu\in D(\mathcal{F}).

According to the notation introduced in (
3.14
), we set

(5.1)

-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}[\mu]=J_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}[\mu],\quad J(x,v)=(x,-v),

and we have
the following result.

Theorem 5.1
.

If
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
is a proper, lower semicontinuous and
\lambda
-convex functional,
then

-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}
is a
(-\lambda)
-dissipative

MPVF
.

Referring to
[
AGS08
]
, here we list interesting and explicit examples of

(-\lambda)
-dissipative MPVFs
induced by proper, lower semicontinuous and
\lambda
-convex functionals,
focusing on the cases
when
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

(1)

Potential energy.
Let
P:\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be
a l.s.c. and
\lambda
-convex functional
satisfying

|\partial^{o}P(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)\quad\text{for every }x\in\mathsf{X},

for some constant
C>0
,
where
\partial^{o}P(x)
is the element of minimal norm in
\partial P(x)
.
By
[
AGS08
, Proposition 10.4.2]

the PVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},-\partial^{o}P)_{\sharp}\mu,\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),

is a
(-\lambda)
-dissipative selection of

-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}_{P}
for the
potential energy functional

\mathcal{F}_{P}(\mu):=\int_{\mathsf{X}}P\,\mathrm{d}\mu,\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

(2)

Interaction energy.

If
W:\mathsf{X}\to[0,+\infty)
is an even, differentiable, and
\lambda
-convex
function for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, whose differential has a linear growth,
then, by
[
AGS08
, Theorem 10.4.11]
, the PVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},(-\nabla W\ast\mu))_{\sharp}\mu,\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),

is a
(-\lambda)
-dissipative selection of
-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}_{W}
,
the opposite of the Wasserstein subdifferential of
the interaction energy functional

\mathcal{F}_{W}(\mu):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathsf{X}^{2}}W(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}(\mu\otimes\mu)(x,y),\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

(3)

Opposite Wasserstein distance.
Let
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
be fixed and consider the functional
\mathcal{F}_{\text{Wass}}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

\mathcal{F}_{\text{Wass}}(\mu):=-\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\bar{\mu}),\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),

which is geodesically
(-1)
-convex
[
AGS08
, Proposition 9.3.12]
.
Setting

\boldsymbol{b}(\mu):=\argmin\left\{\int_{\mathsf{X}}|\boldsymbol{b}(x)-x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu:\boldsymbol{b}=\boldsymbol{b}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X}),\ \boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\bar{\mu})\right\},

the PVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}}-\boldsymbol{b}(\mu))_{\#}\mu,\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

is a selection of
-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}_{\text{Wass}}(\mu)
and it is therefore

1
-dissipative.

5.2.
MPVF concentrated on the graph of a multifunction

The previous example of Section
5.1
has a natural generalization in terms of dissipative graphs in
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}

[
AC84
,
AF09
,
Bré73
]
.
We consider a (not empty)
\lambda
-dissipative set

F\subset\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}
, i.e. satisfying

\langle v_{0}-v_{1},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\leq\lambda|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\quad\text{for any }(x_{0},v_{0}),\ (x_{1},v_{1})\in F.

The corresponding MPVF defined as

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}:=\left\{\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})\mid\Phi\text{ is concentrated on }F\right\}

is
\lambda
-dissipative as well.
In fact, if

\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with
\nu_{i}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{i}
,
i=0,1
,
and
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})

then
(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1},v_{1})\in F\times F

\boldsymbol{\Theta}
-a.e., so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle v_{0}-v_{1},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1},v_{1})\leq\lambda\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\nu_{0},\nu_{1}).

since
(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Gamma_{o}(\nu_{0},\nu_{1})
.
Taking the supremum w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
we
obtain
\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{l}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\nu_{0},\nu_{1})

which is even stronger than
\lambda
-dissipativity.
If
\mathrm{D}(F)=\mathsf{X}

then
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
contains
\mathcal{P}_{\rm c}(\mathsf{X})
, the set of Borel probability measures with compact support.
If
F
has also a linear growth, then it is easy to check that

\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
as well.

Despite the analogy just shown with dissipative operators in Hilbert
spaces, there are important differences with the Wasserstein
framework, as highlighted in the following examples. The main point here is that the dissipativity property
of Definition
4.3
does not force
the sections
\mathsf{v}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
to belong to the tangent space

\Tan_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
.

Example 5.2
.

Let
\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
let
B:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid|x|\leq 1\}
be the closed unit ball,
let
{\mathcal{L}_{B}}
be the (normalized) Lebesgue measure on
B
,
and let
{\boldsymbol{r}}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
{\boldsymbol{r}}(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{2},-x_{1})
be the
anti-clockwise rotation of
\pi/2
degrees. We define the MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]=\begin{cases}(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}},0)_{\sharp}\nu,\quad&\text{ if }\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\setminus\{{\mathcal{L}_{B}}\},\\
\left\{(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}},a{\boldsymbol{r}})_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}_{B}}\mid a\in\mathbb{R}\right\},\quad&\text{ if }\nu={\mathcal{L}_{B}}.\end{cases}

Observe that
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is obviously unbounded at
\nu={\mathcal{L}_{B}}
.
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
also satisfies (
4.2
) with
\lambda=0
(hence it is dissipative): it is enough to check that

(5.2)

\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}},a{\boldsymbol{r}})_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}_{B}},\nu\right]_{r}=0\quad\text{ for
every }\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\,a\in\mathbb{R}.

To prove (
5.2
), we notice that the optimal transport plan from

{\mathcal{L}_{B}}
to
\nu
is concentrated on a map and optimal maps belong to
the tangent space
\Tan_{{\mathcal{L}_{B}}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

[
AGS08
, Prop. 8.5.2]
; by Remark

3.18

we have just to check that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle{\boldsymbol{r}}(x),\nabla\varphi(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{L}_{B}}(x)=0\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

that is a consequence of the Divergence Theorem on
B
. This example
is in contrast with the Hilbertian theory of dissipative operators
according to which an everywhere defined dissipative operator is locally bounded (see
[
Bré73
, Proposition 2.9]
).

Example 5.3
.

In the same setting of the previous example, let us define the MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}},{\boldsymbol{r}})_{\sharp}\nu,\quad{\boldsymbol{r}}(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{2},-x_{1}),\quad\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

It is easy to check that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is dissipative and Lipschitz
continuous (as a map from
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathrm{T}\mathbb{R}^{2})
). Moreover, arguing as in Example

5.2
, we can show that
(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}},0)_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}_{B}}\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[{\mathcal{L}_{B}}]
, where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is defined in
(
4.14
). This is again in contrast with the Hilbertian
theory of dissipative operators, stating that a single valued,
everywhere defined, and continuous dissipative operator coincides with
its
maximal extension (see
[
Bré73
, Proposition 2.4]
).

5.3.
Interaction field induced by a dissipative map

Let us consider the Hilbert space
\mathsf{Y}=\mathsf{X}^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, endowed with the scalar product

\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle x_{i},y_{i}\rangle
,
for every
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n},\ \boldsymbol{y}=(y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathsf{X}^{n}
.
We identify
\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptY}
with
(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})^{n}

and we denote by
\mathsf{x}^{i},\mathsf{v}^{i}
the
i
-th coordinate maps.
Every permutation
\sigma:\{1,\cdots,n\}\to\{1,\cdots,n\}
in
\mathrm{Sym}(n)

operates on
\mathsf{Y}

by the obvious formula

\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})_{i}=x_{\sigma(i)}
,
i=1,\cdots,n
,

\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathsf{Y}
.

Let
G:\mathsf{Y}\to\mathsf{Y}
be a Borel
\lambda
-dissipative
map bounded on bounded sets (this property is always true
if
\mathsf{Y}
has finite dimension) and satisfying

(5.3)

{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathrm{D}(G)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\sigma({\boldsymbol{x}})\in\mathrm{D}(G),\ G(\sigma({\boldsymbol{x}}))=\sigma(G({\boldsymbol{x}}))\quad\text{for every permutation $\sigma$}.

Denoting by
(G^{1},\cdots,G^{n})
the components of
G
, by
\mathsf{x}^{i}
the projections from
\mathsf{Y}
to
\mathsf{X}
and by
\mu^{\otimes n}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mu
,
the MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:=(\mathsf{x}^{1},G^{1})_{\sharp}\mu^{\otimes n}\quad\text{with domain }\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}):=\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathsf{X})

is
\lambda
-dissipative as well.
In fact, if
\mu,\nu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,

\Phi=(\mathsf{x}^{1},G^{1})_{\sharp}\mu^{\otimes n}

and
\Psi=(\mathsf{x}^{1},G^{1})_{\sharp}\nu^{\otimes n}
,
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)
, we can
consider the plan

\boldsymbol{\beta}:=P_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\otimes n}\in\Gamma(\mu^{\otimes n},\nu^{\otimes n})
,
where
P((x_{1},y_{1}),\cdots,(x_{n},y_{n})):=((x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}),(y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}))
.
Considering the map

H^{1}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}):=(x_{1},G^{1}({\boldsymbol{x}}),y_{1},G^{1}({\boldsymbol{y}}))

we have

\boldsymbol{\Theta}:=H^{1}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\Lambda(\Phi,\Psi)
, so that

\displaystyle\left[\Phi,\Psi\right]_{r}

\displaystyle\leq\int\langle v_{1}-w_{1},x_{1}-y_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}(x_{1},v_{1},y_{1},w_{1})=\int\langle G^{1}({\boldsymbol{x}})-G^{1}({\boldsymbol{y}}),x_{1}-y_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int\langle G^{k}({\boldsymbol{x}})-G^{k}({\boldsymbol{y}}),x_{k}-y_{k}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}})=\int\langle G({\boldsymbol{x}})-G({\boldsymbol{y}}),{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}})

where we used (
5.3
) and the invariance of

\boldsymbol{\beta}
with respect to permutations.
The
\lambda
-dissipativity of
G
then yields

\displaystyle\int\langle G({\boldsymbol{x}})-G({\boldsymbol{y}}),{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\int|{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{y}}|^{2}_{\mathsf{Y}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}})=\lambda\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int|x_{k}-y_{k}|^{2}_{\mathsf{Y}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}})

\displaystyle=\lambda\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int|x_{k}-y_{k}|^{2}_{\mathsf{Y}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x_{k},y_{k})=\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu).

A typical example when
n=2
is provided by

G(x_{1},x_{2}):=(A(x_{1}-x_{2}),A(x_{2}-x_{1}))

where
A:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{X}
is a Borel, locally bounded, dissipative
and antisymmetric map
satisfying
A(-z)=-A(z)
.
We easily get

\displaystyle\langle

\displaystyle G({\boldsymbol{x}})-G({\boldsymbol{y}}),{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\langle A(x_{1}-x_{2})-A(y_{1}-y_{2}),x_{1}-y_{1}\rangle-\langle A(x_{1}-x_{2})-A(y_{1}-y_{2}),x_{2}-y_{2}\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle A(x_{1}-x_{2})-A(y_{1}-y_{2}),x_{1}-x_{2}-(y_{1}-y_{2})\rangle\leq 0.

In this case

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{a}[\mu])_{\sharp}\mu,\quad\boldsymbol{a}[\mu](x)=\int_{\mathsf{X}}A(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\quad\text{for every }x\in\mathsf{X}.

6.
Solutions to Measure Differential Inclusions

6.1.
Metric characterization and EVI

Let
\mathcal{I}
denote
an arbitrary (bounded or unbounded) interval in
\mathbb{R}
.

The aim of this section is to study a suitable notion of solution to
the following
differential inclusion in the
L^{2}
-Wasserstein space of probability measures

(6.1)

\dot{\mu}(t)\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu(t)],\qquad t\in\mathcal{I},

driven by a MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
as in Definition
4.1
. In
particular, we will address the usual Cauchy problem when (
6.1
)
is supplemented by a given initial condition.

Measure Differential Inclusions have been introduced in
[
Pic18
]
extending to the multi-valued framework the theory of Measure Differential Equations developed in
[
Pic19
]
. In these papers, the author aims to describe the evolution of curves in the space of probability measures under the action of a so called
probability vector field

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
(see Definition
4.1
and Remark
4.2
). However, as exploited also in
[
Cam+21
]
,
the definition of solution to (
6.1
) given in
[
Pic19
,
Pic18
,
Cam+21
]
is too weak and it does not enjoy uniqueness property which is recovered only at the level of the semigroup through an approximation procedure.

From the Wasserstein viewpoint, the simplest way to interpret (
6.1
) is
to ask for a
locally absolutely continuous curve

\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

to satisfy

(6.2)

(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}[\mu_{t}]\quad\text{for a.e. }t\in\mathcal{I},

where
\boldsymbol{v}
is the Wasserstein metric velocity vector associated to
\mu
(see Theorem
2.10
).
Even in the case of a regular PVF, however, (
6.2
) is too strong, since there is no reason why
a given
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]
should be associated to a vector field of the tangent space
\Tan_{\mu_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
.
Starting from (
6.2
), we thus introduce a weaker definition of solution to
(
6.1
),
modeled on the so-called EVI formulation for gradient flows,
which will eventually suggest, as a natural formulation of (
6.1
), the relaxed version of (
6.2
)
as a differential inclusion with respect to
the extension
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
introduced in (
4.14
).

We start from this simple remark:
whenever
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative, recalling Theorem

3.11
and Remark
4.5
, one easily sees that
every locally absolutely continuous solution according to the above definition (
6.2
) also satisfies
the Evolution Variational Inequality (
\lambda
-EVI)

(
\lambda
-EVI)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)-\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}\quad\text{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}\big(\operatorname{int}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)\big),

for every
\nu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and every
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]
,
where
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{r}
is the functional pairing in Definition

3.5
(in fact, (

λ

-EVI
) holds a.e. in

\mathcal{I}
).
This provides a heuristic motivation for the following definition.

Definition 6.1

(
\lambda
-Evolution Variational Inequality)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a MPVF and let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
We say that a continuous curve
\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
to (
6.1
) for the MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
if
(

λ

-EVI
)
holds for every
\nu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and every
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]
.

A
\lambda
-EVI solution
\mu
is said to be a
strict solution
if
\mu_{t}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
for every
t\in\mathcal{I}
,

t>\inf\mathcal{I}
.

A
\lambda
-EVI solution
\mu
is said to be a
global solution
if
\sup\mathcal{I}=+\infty
.

In Example
6.32
we will clarify the interest in imposing no
more than continuity in the above definition.

Recall that the right
upper and lower Dini derivatives of
a function
\zeta:\mathcal{I}\to\mathbb{R}
are defined for every
t\in\mathcal{I}
,
t<\sup\mathcal{I}
by

(6.3)

{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}\zeta(t)\limsup_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{\zeta(t+h)-\zeta(t)}{h},\qquad{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}_{\kern-1.0pt+}\zeta(t)\liminf_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{\zeta(t+h)-\zeta(t)}{h}.

Remark 6.2
.

Arguing as in
[
MS20
, Lemma A.1]
and
using the lower semicontinuity of the map
t\mapsto\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}
,
the distributional inequality of (

λ

-EVI
)
can be equivalently reformulated
in terms of the right upper or lower Dini derivatives
of the squared distance function
and requiring the condition to hold for every

t\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
:

(
\lambda
-EVI
1
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)-\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}

\displaystyle\text{for every }t\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathcal{I}\right),\ \Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\ \nu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi,

(
\lambda
-EVI
2
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}_{\kern-1.0pt+}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)-\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}

\displaystyle\text{for every }t\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathcal{I}\right),\ \Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\ \nu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi.

A further equivalent formulation

[
MS20
, Theorem 3.3]

involves the difference quotients:
for every
s,t\in\mathcal{I}
,
s<t

(
\lambda
-EVI
3
)

{\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda(t-s)}}\,W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)-W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s},\nu)\leq-2\int_{s}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda(r-s)}\left[\Phi,\mu_{r}\right]_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\quad\text{for every }\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\ \nu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi.

Finally,
if
\mu
is also locally absolutely continuous, then
(

λ

-EVI1
) and (

λ

-EVI2
) are also equivalent to

\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)&\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)-\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}\end{aligned}\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and every}\ \Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\ \nu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi.

The following Lemma provides a further insight.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a

\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
and let
\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

be a continuous
\lambda
-
EVI
solution to (
6.1
).
We have

(6.4a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}\quad

for every
\nu\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
,
t\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
,

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{t}},{\nu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,

(6.4b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)+[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}\quad

for every
\nu\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
,
t\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathcal{I}\right),

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{1}({\mu_{t}},{\nu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.

If moreover
\mu
is locally absolutely continuous
with Wasserstein velocity
field

\boldsymbol{v}
satisfying (
2.6
)
for every
t
in the subset
A(\mu)\subset\mathcal{I}
of full
Lebesgue measure,
then

(6.5a)

\displaystyle\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\nu\right]_{r}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)-\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}

\displaystyle\text{if $t\in A(\mu)$,\ $\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\ \nu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi$},

(6.5b)

\displaystyle[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}

\displaystyle\text{if $t\in A(\mu)$, }\nu\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})},\ \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{t}},{\nu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),

(6.5c)

\displaystyle[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)+[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{1-}

\displaystyle\text{if $t\in A(\mu)$, }\nu\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})},\ \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{1}({\mu_{t}},{\nu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}).

Proof.

In order to check (
6.5a
) it is sufficient to
combine (
3.19
) of Theorem
3.11

with
(

λ

-EVI1
).
(
6.5b
) and (
6.5c
) then follow applying Proposition
4.14
.
Let us now prove (
6.4a
):
let us fix
\nu\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
and
t\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.
Take
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t},\nu)

and define the constant speed geodesic

(\nu_{s})_{s\in[0,1]}
by
\nu_{s}:=(\mathsf{x}^{s})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
thus in particular
\nu_{0}=\mu_{t}
and
\nu_{1}=\nu
. Then by Lemma

2.11
, for every

s\in\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\cap(0,1)

and
\Phi_{s}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}(\nu_{s})
we have

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)&\leq\frac{1}{2s}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu_{s})\leq-\frac{1}{s}\left[\Phi_{s},\mu_{t}\right]_{r}+\frac{\lambda}{s}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu_{s})\\
&\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}+\lambda sW_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu),\end{split}

where the second inequality comes
from (

λ

-EVI1
).
Taking
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{t}},{\nu}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and passing to the limit as
s\downarrow 0
we get
(
6.4a
). Analogously for (
6.4b
).
∎

We can now give an interpretation of
absolutely continuous
\lambda
-EVI solutions
in terms of differential inclusions.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a

\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
and let
\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
be a locally absolutely
continuous
curve.

(1)

If
\mu
satisfies the differential
inclusion (
6.2
) driven by any
\lambda
-dissipative
extension of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
in

{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
,
then
\mu
is also a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution to (
6.1
) for
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

(2)

\mu
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
of (
6.1
) for
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

if and only if

(6.6)

(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}[\mu_{t}]\quad\text{for a.e. }t\in\mathcal{I}.

(3)

If
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
satisfies (
4.13
) and

\mu_{t}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

for a.e.
t\in\mathcal{I}
,
then the following properties are equivalent:

-

\mu
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution to (
6.1
) for
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

-

\mu
satisfies (
6.5b
).

-

\mu
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution to (
6.1
) for the restriction of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
to
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.

(4)

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
satisfies (
4.16
)
then

\mu
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution to (
6.1
) for
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
if and only if
it is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution to (
6.1
) for
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

Proof.

(1)
It is sufficient to apply Theorem
3.11

and the definition of
\lambda
-dissipativity.

The left-to-right implication
\Rightarrow

of (2) follows by (
6.5a
) of Lemma
6.3

and the definition of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

Conversely, if

\mu
satisfies (
6.6
),
\nu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]
,
then Theorem

3.11

and the definition of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

yield

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)

\displaystyle=\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\nu\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)-\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}\quad\text{a.e.\penalty\ in }\mathcal{I}.

Claim (3) is an immediate consequence of Lemma
6.3
, Proposition
4.17
(d) and Proposition
4.14
.

Claim (4) is a consequence of Proposition
4.17
(f) and the
\lambda
-dissipativity of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
be a proper, lower semicontinuous and
\lambda
-convex functional and let
\mu\in\mathrm{C}(\mathcal{I};\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}))
be a locally absolutely continuous curve. Then

(1)

if
\mu
is a Gradient Flow for
\mathcal{F}
i.e.

(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}(\mu_{t})\quad\text{ a.e. }t\in\mathcal{I},

then
\mu
is a
(-\lambda)
-
EVI
solution of (
6.1
) for the
MPVF

-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}
as in (
5.1
);

(2)

if
\mu
is a
(-\lambda)
-
EVI
solution of (
6.1
) for the
MPVF

-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}
and the domain of
\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}
satisfies

\text{ for a.e. }t\in\mathcal{I},\,\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{t}},{\nu}|\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F})\neq\emptyset\quad\forall\nu\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}),

then
\mu
is a Gradient Flow for
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

The first assertion is a consequence Theorem
6.4
(1). We prove the second claim; by (
6.5b
) we have that for a.e.
t\in\mathcal{I}
it holds

\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\nu\right]_{r}\leq[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq[-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{0+}\quad\forall\,\nu\in\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{F})\,\,\forall\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{t}},{\nu}|\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}).

We show that for every
\nu_{0},\nu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F})
and every
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\nu_{0}},{\nu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

(6.7)

[-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F},\boldsymbol{\nu}]_{0+}\leq\mathcal{F}(\nu_{1})-\mathcal{F}(\nu_{0})-\frac{\lambda}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\nu_{0},\nu_{1}).

To prove that, we take
s\in\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{\nu}|\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F})\cap(0,1)
and
\Phi_{s}\in-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{s})
. By definition of subdifferential we have

\left[\Phi_{s},\nu_{1}\right]_{r}\leq\mathcal{F}(\nu_{1})-\mathcal{F}(\nu_{s})-\frac{\lambda}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\nu_{s},\nu_{1})

where
\nu_{s}=\mathsf{x}^{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\nu}
. Dividing by
(1-s)
, using (
3.26
) and passing to the infimum w.r.t.
\Phi_{s}\in-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{s})
we obtain

[-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F},\boldsymbol{\nu}]_{r,s}\leq\frac{1}{1-s}\left(\mathcal{F}(\nu_{1})-\mathcal{F}(\nu_{s})\right)-\frac{\lambda(1-s)}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\nu_{0},\nu_{1}).

Passing to the limit as
s\downarrow 0
and using the lower semicontinuity of
\mathcal{F}
lead to the result. Once that (
6.7
) is established we have that for a.e.
t\in\mathcal{I}
it holds

(6.8)

\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\nu\right]_{r}\leq\mathcal{F}(\nu)-\mathcal{F}(\mu_{t})-\frac{\lambda}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)\text{ for every }\nu\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}).

To conclude it is enough to use the lower semicontinuity of the LHS (see Lemma
3.14
) and the fact that
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F})
is dense in
\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{F})
in energy: indeed we can apply
[
NS21
, Corollary 4.5]
and
[
AGS08
, Lemma 3.1.2]
to the proper, lower semicontinuous and convex functional
\mathcal{F}^{\lambda}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
defined as

\mathcal{F}^{\lambda}(\nu)=\mathcal{F}(\nu)-\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\nu)

to get the existence, for every
\nu\in\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{F})
, of a family
(\nu_{\tau})_{\tau>0}\subset\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{F}^{\lambda})=\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{F})
s.t.

\nu_{\tau}\to\nu,\quad\mathcal{F}^{\lambda}(\nu_{\tau})\to\mathcal{F}^{\lambda}(\nu)\quad\text{ as }\tau\downarrow 0.

Of course
\mathcal{F}(\nu_{\tau})\to\mathcal{F}(\nu)
as
\tau\downarrow 0
and, applying
[
AGS08
, Lemma 10.3.4]
, we see that
\nu_{\tau}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}^{\lambda})
. However
\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}^{\lambda}=L^{\lambda}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}
(see (
4.5
)) so that
\nu_{\tau}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F})
. We can thus write (
6.8
) for
\nu_{\tau}
in place of
\nu
and pass to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0
, obtaining that, by definition of subdifferential,
(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}(\mu_{t})
for a.e.
t\in\mathcal{I}
.
∎

We derive a further useful a priori bound for
\lambda
-EVI solutions.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
and let
T\in(0,+\infty]
.
Every
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
\mu:[0,T)\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

with initial datum
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
satisfies the a priori bound

(6.9)

W_{2}(\mu_{t},\mu_{0})\leq 2|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu_{0})\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad\text{for all }t\in[0,T),

where

|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu):={}\inf\Big\{|\Phi|_{2}:\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]\Big\}\quad\text{for every }\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}).

Proof.

Let
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}(\mu_{0})
. (

λ

-EVI
) with
\nu:=\mu_{0}
then
yields

{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\mu_{0})-2\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\mu_{0})\leq-2\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}\leq 2|\Phi|_{2}\,W_{2}(\mu_{t},\mu_{0}),\quad\text{for every }t\in[0,T).

We can then apply the estimate of
[
AGS08
, Lemma 4.1.8]
to obtain

\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}W_{2}(\mu_{t},\mu_{0})\leq 2|\Phi|_{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad\text{for all }t\in[0,T),

which in turn yields (
6.9
).
∎

We conclude this section with a result showing the robustness of the notion of
\lambda
-EVI solution.

Proposition 6.7
.

If
\mu_{n}:\mathcal{I}\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
is a sequence of

\lambda
-
EVI
solutions locally uniformly converging to
\mu
as
n\to\infty
,
then
\mu
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution.

Proof.

\mu
is a continuous curve defined in
\mathcal{I}
with values in
\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
.
Using pointwise convergence, the lower semicontinuity of

\mu\mapsto\left[\Phi,\mu\right]_{r}
of Lemma
3.14
, and Fatou’s Lemma,
it is easy to pass to the limit in the equivalent characterization (

λ

-EVI3
)
of
\lambda
-EVI solutions, written for
\mu_{n}
.
∎

6.2.
Local existence of
\lambda
-EVI solutions by the Explicit Euler Scheme

In order to prove the existence of a
\lambda
-EVI solution to (
6.1
), our strategy is to employ an approximation argument through an Explicit Euler scheme as it occurs for ODEs.

In the following
\left\lfloor\cdot\right\rfloor
and
\left\lceil\cdot\right\rceil
denote the floor and the ceiling functions respectively.

Definition 6.8

(Explicit Euler Scheme)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a MPVF and suppose we are given
a step size
\tau>0
, an initial datum
\mu^{0}_{\tau}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
a bounded interval
[0,T]
, corresponding to
the final step
{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)}:=\left\lceil T/\tau\right\rceil,

and a stability bound
L>0
.
A sequence

(M^{n}_{\tau},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n})_{0\leq n\leq{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)}}\subset\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\times{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a

L
-stable solution to the Explicit Euler Scheme in
[0,T]
starting from
