\mu^{0}_{\tau}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
if

(EE)

\left\{\begin{aligned} M_{\tau}^{0}&=\mu^{0}_{\tau},\\
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n}&\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[M_{\tau}^{n}],\ |{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n}|_{2}\leq L&&0\leq n<{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)},\\
M_{\tau}^{n}&=(\operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{\tau})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n-1}&&1\leq n\leq{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)}.\end{aligned}\right.

We define the following two different interpolations of the sequence
(M^{n}_{\tau},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n})
:

•

the affine interpolation:

(6.10)

M_{\tau}(t):=(\operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{t-n\tau})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n}\text{\quad if }t\in[n\tau,(n+1)\tau]\text{ for some }n\in\mathbb{N},\ 0\leq n<{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)},

•

the piecewise constant interpolation:

\begin{split}\bar{M}_{\tau}(t)&:=M^{\left\lfloor t/\tau\right\rfloor}_{\tau},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(t)&:={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\left\lfloor t/\tau\right\rfloor}_{\tau},\quad t\in[0,T].\end{split}

We will call
\mathscr{E}(\mu^{0}_{\tau},\tau,T,L)
(resp.
\mathscr{M}(\mu^{0}_{\tau},\tau,T,L)
)
the (possibly empty)
set of all the curves
(M_{\tau},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau})
(resp.
M_{\tau}
) arising from the
solution of (
EE
).

The affine interpolation can be trivially written as

M_{\tau}(t)=\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{t-\left\lfloor t/\tau\right\rfloor\tau}\right)_{\sharp}\left({\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(t)\right),\quad t\in[0,T],

and
M_{\tau}
satisfies the uniform Lipschitz bound

(6.11)

W_{2}(M_{\tau}(t),M_{\tau}(s))\leq L|t-s|\quad 0\leq s\leq t\leq T,\quad M_{\tau}\in\mathscr{E}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L).

Notice that, since in general
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
is not reduced to a singleton,
the sets
\mathscr{E}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)
and
\mathscr{M}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)

may contain more than one element (or may be empty).
Stable solutions to the Explicit Euler scheme generated by a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF exhibit a nice behaviour, which is clarified by the following important result,
which will be proved in Section
7
(see Proposition

7.3
and Theorems
7.4
,

7.5
,
7.7
), with a more accurate
estimate of the error constants
A(\vartheta)
.
We stress that in the next statement

A(\vartheta)
solely depend on
\vartheta

(in particular, it is independent of
\lambda,L,T,\tau,\eta,M_{\tau},M_{\eta}
).

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a

\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
.

(1)

For every

\mu_{0},\mu_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
every

M_{\tau}\in\mathscr{M}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)
,
M_{\tau}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{M}(\mu_{0}^{\prime},\tau,T,L)

with
\tau\lambda_{+}\leq 2
we have

(6.12)

W_{2}(M_{\tau}(t),M_{\tau}^{\prime}(t))\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{0}^{\prime})+8L\sqrt{t\tau}\Big(1+|\lambda|\sqrt{t\tau}\Big)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}t}\quad\text{for every }t\in[0,T].

(2)

For every
\vartheta>1

there exists a constant
A(\vartheta)
such that
if
M_{\tau}\in\mathscr{M}(M^{0}_{\tau},\tau,T,L)

and
M_{\eta}\in\mathscr{M}(M^{0}_{\eta},\eta,T,L)

with
\lambda_{+}(\tau+\eta)\leq 1
then

W_{2}(M_{\tau}(t),M_{\eta}(t))\leq\Big(\vartheta W_{2}(M^{0}_{\tau},M^{0}_{\eta})+A(\vartheta)L\sqrt{(\tau+\eta)(t+\tau+\eta)}\Big)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}\,t},\quad t\in[0,T].

(3)

For every
\vartheta>1

there exists a constant
A(\vartheta)

such that
if
\mu\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})})
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
and
M_{\tau}\in\mathscr{M}(M^{0}_{\tau},\tau,T,L)

then

(6.13)

W_{2}(\mu(t),M_{\tau}(t))\leq\Big(\vartheta\,W_{2}(\mu_{0},M^{0}_{\tau})+A(\vartheta)L\sqrt{\tau(t+\tau)}\Big)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}t}\quad\text{for every }t\in[0,T].

(4)

If
n\mapsto\tau(n)
is a vanishing sequence of
time steps,
(\mu_{0,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
converging to

\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and

M_{n}\in\mathscr{M}(\mu_{0,n},\tau(n),T,L)
,
then
M_{n}
is uniformly converging to a limit curve

\mu\in\mathrm{Lip}([0,T];\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})})
which
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
starting from
\mu_{0}
.

If we assume that the Explicit Euler scheme is locally solvable,
Theorem
6.9

provides a crucial tool to obtain local existence and uniqueness of
\lambda
-EVI solutions.

Definition 6.10

(Local and global solvability of (
EE
))
.

We say that the Explicit Euler Scheme (
EE
) associated to a MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

is
locally solvable

at
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
if there exist strictly positive constants

\boldsymbol{\tau},T,L
such that

\mathscr{E}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)
is not empty for every
\tau\in(0,\boldsymbol{\tau})
.

We say that (
EE
) is
globally solvable
at

\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
if for every
T>0
there exist strictly positive constants

\boldsymbol{\tau},L
such that

\mathscr{E}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)
is not empty for every
\tau\in(0,\boldsymbol{\tau})
.

Let us now state the main existence result for

\lambda
-EVI solutions. Given
T\in(0,+\infty]
and
\mu:[0,T)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
we
denote by
|\dot{\mu}|_{+}(t)
the right upper metric derivative

|\dot{\mu}|_{+}(t):=\limsup_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{W_{2}(\mu_{t+h},\mu_{t})}{h}.

Theorem 6.11

(Local existence and uniqueness)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
.

(a)

If
the Explicit Euler Scheme is locally solvable at

\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, then there exists
T>0

and a unique
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
\mu\in\mathrm{Lip}([0,T];\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})})
starting from
\mu_{0}
,
satisfying

(6.14)

t\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}|\dot{\mu}|_{+}(t)\quad\text{is decreasing in }[0,T).

If
\mu^{\prime}:[0,T^{\prime}]\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
is any other
\lambda
-
EVI
solution starting from
\mu_{0}

then
\mu(t)=\mu^{\prime}(t)
if
0\leq t\leq T\land T^{\prime}
.

(b)

If
the Explicit Euler Scheme is locally solvable in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and

(6.15)

\begin{gathered}\text{for any local $\lambda$-{\rm EVI} solution $\mu$ starting from $\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})$}\\
\text{there exists }\delta>0:\quad t\in[0,\delta]\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mu(t)\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),\end{gathered}

then for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
there exist a unique
maximal time
T\in(0,\infty]
and a
unique strict
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
\mu\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\rm loc}([0,T);\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}))
starting from
\mu_{0}
,
which satisfies (
6.14
) and

(6.16)

T<\infty\quad\Rightarrow\quad\lim_{t\uparrow T}\mu_{t}\not\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}).

Any other
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
\mu^{\prime}:[0,T^{\prime})\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

starting from
\mu_{0}
coincides with
\mu

in
[0,T\land T^{\prime})
.

Proof.

(a)
Let
\boldsymbol{\tau},T,L
positive constants such that

\mathscr{E}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)
is not empty for every
\tau\in(0,\boldsymbol{\tau})
.
Thanks to Theorem
6.9
(2),
the family
M_{\tau}\in\mathscr{E}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)

satisfies the Cauchy condition in
\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}))

so that there exists a unique limit curve
\mu=\lim_{\tau\downarrow 0}M_{\tau}

which is also Lipschitz in time, thanks to the a-priori bound
(
6.11
).
Theorem
6.9
(4) shows that
\mu
is a
\lambda
-EVI solution
starting from
\mu_{0}
and
the estimate (
6.13
) of Theorem
6.9
(3)
shows that any other
\lambda
-EVI solution
in an interval
[0,T^{\prime}]
starting from
\mu_{0}
should coincide with

\mu
in
[0,T^{\prime}\land T]
.

Let us now check (
6.14
): we fix
s,t
such that
0\leq s<t<T

and
h\in(0,T-t)
,
and we set
s_{\tau}:=\tau\left\lfloor s/\tau\right\rfloor
,
h_{\tau}:=\tau\left\lfloor h/\tau\right\rfloor
.
The curves
r\mapsto M_{\tau}(s_{\tau}+r)
,

r\mapsto M_{\tau}(s_{\tau}+h_{\tau}+r)
belong
to
\mathscr{M}(M_{\tau}(s_{\tau}),\tau,t-s,L)
and

\mathscr{M}(M_{\tau}(s_{\tau}+h_{\tau}),\tau,t-s,L)
, so that (
6.12
) yields

W_{2}(M_{\tau}(s_{\tau}+t-s),M_{\tau}(s_{\tau}+h_{\tau}+(t-s)))\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda(t-s)}W_{2}(M_{\tau}(s_{\tau}),M_{\tau}(s_{\tau}+h_{\tau}))+B\sqrt{\tau},

for
B=B(\lambda,L,\boldsymbol{\tau},T)
.
Passing to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0
we get

W_{2}(\mu(t),\mu(t+h))\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda(t-s)}W_{2}(\mu(s),\mu(s+h)).

Dividing by
h
and passing to the limit as
h\downarrow 0
we get
(
6.14
).

(b)
Let us call
\mathcal{S}
the collection of

\lambda
-EVI solutions
\mu:[0,S)\to\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

starting from
\mu_{0}
with values in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and defined in some interval

[0,S)
,
S=S(\mu)
. Thanks to (
6.15
) and the previous claim
the set
\mathcal{S}
is not empty.

It is also easy to check that two curves
\mu^{\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}

coincide
in the common domain
[0,S)
with
S:=S(\mu^{\prime})\land S(\mu^{\prime\prime})
:
in fact the set
\{t\in[0,S):\mu^{\prime}(r)=\mu^{\prime\prime}(r)\text{ if }0\leq r\leq t\}
contains
t=0
,
is closed since
\mu^{\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime}
are continuous, and it is also open
since if
\mu^{\prime}=\mu^{\prime\prime}
in
[0,t]
then
the previous claim and the fact that
\mu^{\prime}(t)=\mu^{\prime\prime}(t)\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

show that
\mu^{\prime}=\mu^{\prime\prime}
also in a right neighborhood of
t
.
Since
[0,S)
is connected, we conclude that
\mu^{\prime}=\mu^{\prime\prime}
in
[0,S)
.

We can thus define
T:=\sup\{S(\mu):\mu\in\mathcal{S}\}

obtaining that there exists a unique
\lambda
-EVI solution

\mu
starting from
\mu_{0}
and defined in
[0,T)

with values in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.

If
T<\infty
, since
\mu
is Lipschitz in
[0,T)
thanks to (
6.14
),
we know that
there exists the limit
\bar{\mu}:=\lim_{t\uparrow T}\mu(t)
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
.
If
\bar{\mu}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
we can extend
\mu
to a
\lambda
-EVI solution
with values in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and defined in an interval
[0,T^{\prime})
with
T^{\prime}>T
,
which contradicts the maximality of
T
.
∎

Recall that
a set
A
in a metric space
X
is locally closed
if every point of
A
has a neighborhood
U
such that

A\cap U=\bar{A}\cap U
. Equivalently,
A
is the intersection of an open and a closed subset of
X
.
In particular, open or closed sets are locally closed.

Corollary 6.12
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
for which the Explicit Euler Scheme is locally solvable
in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.
If
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is locally closed
then for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
there exists a unique
maximal strict
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
\mu\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\rm loc}([0,T);\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}))
,
T\in(0,+\infty]
, satisfying (
6.16
).

Let us briefly discuss
the question of local solvability of the Explicit Euler scheme.
The main constraints of the Explicit Euler construction relies on the
a priori stability bound and in the
condition
M_{\tau}^{n}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
for every step
0\leq n\leq{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)}
.
This constraint is feasible if at each measure
M^{n}_{\tau}
,
0\leq n<{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)}
, the set

\Adm_{\tau,L}(M^{n}_{\tau})
defined by

\Adm_{\tau,L}(\mu):=\Big\{\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:|\Phi|_{2}\leq L\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}_{\sharp}^{\tau}\Phi\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\Big\}

is not empty.
If
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is open and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is locally bounded, then it is easy to check that
the Explicit Euler scheme is locally solvable (see Lemma
6.13
).
We will adopt the following notation:

(6.17)

\displaystyle|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu):={}

\displaystyle\inf\Big\{|\Phi|_{2}:\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]\Big\}\quad\text{for every }\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),

and we will also introduce
the upper semicontinuous envelope
|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2\star}
of
the function
|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}
:
i.e.

\displaystyle|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2\star}(\mu):={}

\displaystyle\inf_{\delta>0}\sup\Big\{|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\nu):\nu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),\ W_{2}(\nu,\mu)\leq\delta\Big\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sup\Big\{\limsup_{k\to\infty}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu_{k}):\mu_{k}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),\ \mu_{k}\to\mu\text{ in
}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\Big\}.

Lemma 6.13
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
,
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{Int}(\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}))

and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is bounded in a neighborhood of
\mu_{0}
,
i.e.
there exists
\varrho>0
such that
|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}
is bounded in
\mathrm{B}(\mu_{0},\varrho)
, then
the Explicit Euler scheme is locally solvable at
\mu_{0}

and the locally Lipschitz solution
\mu
given by Theorem

6.11
(a) satisfies

(6.18)

|\dot{\mu}|_{+}(t)\leq e^{\lambda t}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2\star}(\mu_{0})\quad\forall\,t\in[0,T).

In particular, if
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is open and

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is locally bounded,
for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
there exists a unique
maximal
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
\mu\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\rm loc}([0,T);\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}))

satisfying (
6.16
) and (
6.18
).

Proof.

Let
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{Int}(\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}))
and let
\varrho,L>0
so that

|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu)<L
for every
\mu\in\mathrm{B}(\mu_{0},\varrho)
.
We set

T:=\varrho/(2L)
,
\boldsymbol{\tau}=T\land 1
and we perform
a simple induction argument to prove that

W_{2}(M^{n}_{\tau},\mu_{0})\leq Ln\tau<\varrho
if
n\leq{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)}

so that we can always find an element
{\boldsymbol{F}}^{n}_{\tau}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\tau,L}
.
In fact, if
W_{2}(M^{n}_{\tau},\mu_{0})<Ln\tau
and
n<{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)}

then

W_{2}(M^{n+1}_{\tau},\mu_{0})\leq W_{2}(M^{n+1}_{\tau},M^{n}_{\tau})+W_{2}(M^{n}_{\tau},\mu_{0})\leq L(n+1)\tau
.
(
6.14
) shows that

|\dot{\mu}_{t}|_{+}\leq L\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}

for every
L>|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2\star}(\mu_{0})
, so that we obtain (
6.18
).
∎

More refined estimates will be discussed in the next sections.
Here we will show another example, tailored to the case of measures
with bounded support.

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
such that
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\subset\mathcal{P}_{\rm b}(\mathsf{X})
and
for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
there exist
\varrho>0
,
L>0

such that
for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\rm b}(\mathsf{X})

\supp(\mu)\subset\supp(\mu_{0})+\mathrm{B}_{\mathsf{X}}(\varrho)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\exists\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:\supp(\mathsf{v}_{\sharp}\Phi)\subset\mathrm{B}_{\mathsf{X}}(L).

Then for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
there exists
T\in(0,+\infty]
and a unique
maximal strict
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
\mu\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\rm{loc}}([0,T);\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}))

satisfying (
6.16
).

Proof.

Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
6.13
,
it is easy to check that
setting
T:=\varrho/4L
,
\boldsymbol{\tau}=T\wedge 1

we can find a discrete solution
(M_{\tau},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau})\in\mathscr{E}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)

satisfying the more restrictive condition

\supp(M^{n}_{\tau})\subset\supp(\mu_{0})+\mathrm{B}_{\mathsf{X}}(Ln\tau)\subset\supp(\mu_{0})+\mathrm{B}_{\mathsf{X}}(\varrho/2)
,
\supp(\mathsf{v}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{n}_{\tau})\subset\mathrm{B}_{\mathsf{X}}(L)

so that
the Explicit Euler scheme is locally solvable and
M_{\tau}
satisfies the uniform bound

(6.19)

\supp(M_{\tau}(t))\subset\supp(\mu_{0})+\mathrm{B}_{\mathsf{X}}(\varrho/2)\quad\text{for every }t\in[0,T].

Theorem
6.11
then yields the existence of a local solution, and
Theorem
6.9
(3) shows that the local solution
satisfies the same bound (
6.19
) on the support, so that (
6.15
) holds.
∎

6.3.
Stability and uniqueness

In the following theorem we prove a stability result for
\lambda
-EVI solutions
of (
6.1
), as it occurs in the classical Hilbertian case
scenario. We distinguish three cases: the first one
assumes that the Explicit Euler scheme is locally solvable in

\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.

Theorem 6.15

(Uniqueness and Stability)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
such that the Explicit Euler scheme is locally solvable in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, and
let
\mu^{1},\mu^{2}:[0,T)\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
,
T\in(0,+\infty]
, be

\lambda
-
EVI
solutions to (
6.1
).
If

\mu^{1}
is strict,
then

(6.20)

W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})\leq W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{0},\mu^{2}_{0})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}\,t}\quad\text{for every }t\in[0,T).

In particular, if
\mu^{1}_{0}=\mu^{2}_{0}
then
\mu^{1}\equiv\mu^{2}
in
[0,T)
.

If
\mu^{1},\mu^{2}
are both strict, then

(6.21)

W_{2}(\mu_{t}^{1},\mu_{t}^{2})\leq W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{0},\mu^{2}_{0})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}\quad\text{for all }t\in[0,T).

Proof.

In order to prove (
6.20
),
let us fix
t\in(0,T)
.
Since the Explicit Euler scheme is locally solvable and
\mu^{1}_{t}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
there exist
\boldsymbol{\tau},\delta,L

such that
\mathscr{M}(\mu^{1}_{t},\tau,\delta,L)

is not empty
for every
\tau\in(0,\boldsymbol{\tau})
.
If
M^{1}_{\tau}\in\mathscr{M}(\mu^{1}_{t},\tau,\delta,L)
, then (
6.13
) yields

\displaystyle W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t+h},\mu^{2}_{t+h})

\displaystyle\leq W_{2}(M^{1}_{\tau}(h),\mu^{2}_{t+h})+W_{2}(M^{1}_{\tau}(h),\mu^{1}_{t+h})

\displaystyle\leq\vartheta W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}h}+B\sqrt{\tau}\quad\text{if }0\leq h\leq\delta,

for
B=B(\lambda,L,\boldsymbol{\tau},\delta)

Passing to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0

we obtain

W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t+h},\mu^{2}_{t+h})\leq\vartheta W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}h}

and a further limit as
\vartheta\downarrow 1
yields

W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t+h},\mu^{2}_{t+h})\leq W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}h}\quad\text{for every }h\in[0,\delta],

which implies that the map
t\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{+}t}W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})
is decreasing in
[t,t+\delta]
.
Since
t
is arbitrary, we obtain (
6.20
).

In order to prove the estimate (
6.21
)
(which is better than (
6.20
) when
\lambda<0
),
we argue in a similar way, using (
6.12
).

As before, for a given
t\in(0,T)
,
since the Explicit Euler scheme is locally solvable and
\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
there exist
\boldsymbol{\tau},\delta,L

such that
\mathscr{M}(\mu^{1}_{t},\tau,\delta,L)

and

\mathscr{M}(\mu^{2}_{t},\tau,\delta,L)
are not empty
for every
\tau\in(0,\boldsymbol{\tau})
.
If
M^{i}_{\tau}\in\mathscr{M}(\mu^{i}_{t},\tau,\delta,L)
, for
i=1,2
, (
6.12
) and (
6.13
) then yield

\displaystyle W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t+h},\mu^{2}_{t+h})

\displaystyle\leq W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t+h},M^{1}_{\tau}(h))+W_{2}(M^{1}_{\tau}(h),M^{2}_{\tau}(h))+W_{2}(\mu^{2}_{t+h},M^{2}_{\tau}(h))

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda h}W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})+B\sqrt{\tau}\quad\text{if }0\leq h\leq\delta,

for
B=B(\lambda,L,\boldsymbol{\tau},\delta)
.
Passing to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0

we obtain

W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t+h},\mu^{2}_{t+h})\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda h}W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})

which implies that the map
t\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})
is decreasing in
(0,T)
.
∎

It is possible to prove (
6.21
)
by a direct argument depending on the definition of
\lambda
-EVI solution
and a geometric condition on
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
. The simplest situation
deals with absolutely continuous curves.

Theorem 6.16

(Stability for absolutely continuous solutions)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
and let
\mu^{1},\mu^{2}:[0,T)\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
,
T\in(0,+\infty]
, be

locally absolutely continuous

\lambda
-
EVI
solutions to (
6.1
).
If
\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu^{1}_{t}},{\mu^{2}_{t}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\neq\emptyset
for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, then (
6.21
) holds.
In particular, if
\mu^{1}_{0}=\mu^{2}_{0}
then

\mu^{1}\equiv\mu^{2}
in
[0,T)
.

Proof.

Since
\mu^{1},\mu^{2}
are locally absolutely continuous curves, we can apply
Theorem

3.13
and find a subset
A\subset A({\mu^{1}})\cap A({\mu^{2}})
of full Lebesgue measure such that
(
3.20
) holds and
\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu^{1}_{t}},{\mu^{2}_{t}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\neq\emptyset
for every
t\in A
.
Selecting
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu^{1}_{t}},{\mu^{2}_{t}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, we have

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})=\int\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}^{1}(x_{1}),x_{1}-x_{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})+\int\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}^{2}(x_{2}),x_{2}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2}).

Using
(
6.5b
), (
6.5c
), for every
t\in A
we get

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})=\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}^{1},\mu_{t}^{2}\right]_{r}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}]_{0+}+\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})+[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}]_{1-}=\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t}),

where we also used the property

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}]_{1-}=-[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}]_{0+}.\qed

The last situation deals with comparison between
an absolutely continuous and a merely continuous
\lambda
-EVI
solution.
The argument is technically more involved
and takes inspiration from the proof of
[
NS06
, Theorem
1.1]
:
we refer to the Introduction of
[
NS06
]
for an
explanation of the heuristic idea.
Since it is also at the core of the discrete estimates of Theorem

6.9
, we
present it here in the easier
continuous setting.

Theorem 6.17

(Refined stability)
.

Let
T>0
and let

\mu^{1}\in\mathrm{AC}([0,T];\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}))
and
\mu^{2}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})})
be
\lambda
-
EVI
solutions for the

\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
. If
at least one of the following properties hold:

(1)

\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu^{1}_{r}},{\mu^{2}_{s}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\neq\emptyset\text{
for every }s\in(0,T)\text{ and }r\in[0,T)\setminus N

with
N\subset(0,T),\ \mathcal{L}^{1}(N)=0
;

(2)

\mu^{1}
satisfies (
6.2
),

then

W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})\leq e^{\lambda t}W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{0},\mu^{2}_{0})\quad\text{ for every }t\in[0,T].

Proof.

We extend
\mu^{1}
in
(-\infty,0)
with the constant value

\mu^{1}_{0}
,
we denote by
\boldsymbol{v}
the Wasserstein velocity field
associated to
\mu^{1}
(and extended to
0
outside
A(\mu^{1})
)
and
we define the functions
w,f,h:(-\infty,T]\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
by

w(r,s):=W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{r},\mu^{2}_{s})

f(r,s):=\begin{cases}2|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu_{0}^{1})w(0,s)\quad&\text{ if
}r<0,\\
0&\text{ if }r\geq 0,\end{cases}\qquad h(r,s):=\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{ if
}r<0,\\
2\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{r})_{\sharp}\mu^{1}_{r},\mu^{2}_{s}\right]_{r}&\text{ if
}r\geq 0.\end{cases}

Theorem
3.11
yields

(6.22)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial r}w^{2}(r,s)=h(r,s)\quad

\displaystyle\text{ in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(-\infty,T)\text{ for
every }s\in[0,T].

In case (1) holds,
writing (
6.4b
) for
\mu^{2}
with
\nu=\mu^{1}_{r}

with
r\in(-\infty,T]\setminus N
,
then for every
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{rs}\in\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu^{1}_{r}},{\mu^{2}_{s}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
we obtain

(6.23)

\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}w^{2}(r,s)\leq 2\lambda w^{2}(r,s)-2[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{rs}]_{0+}\quad

\displaystyle\text{ for }s\in(0,T)\text{ and }r\in(-\infty,T)\setminus N.

On the other hand (
6.5b
) yields

(6.24)

\displaystyle-2[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{rs}]_{0+}

\displaystyle\leq-2[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{r})_{\sharp}\mu^{1}_{r},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{rs}]_{r,0}\leq-2\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{r})_{\sharp}\mu^{1}_{r},\mu^{2}_{s}\right]_{r}\quad\text{for
every }r\in A(\mu^{1})\setminus N,

\displaystyle-2[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{rs}]_{0+}

\displaystyle\leq 2|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu_{0}^{1})w(0,s)=f(r,s)\quad\text{for every }r<0.

Combining (
6.3
) and
(
6.24
) we obtain

{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}w^{2}(r,s)\leq 2\lambda w^{2}(r,s)+f(r,s)-h(r,s)\quad\text{for }s\in(0,T),\ r\in(-\infty,0]\cup A(\mu^{1})\setminus N.

Since
|h(r,s)|\leq 2|\dot{\mu}^{1}_{r}|\,w(r,s)
,
applying Lemma
C.2
we get

(6.25)

\frac{\partial}{\partial s}w^{2}(r,s)\leq 2\lambda w^{2}(r,s)+f(r,s)-h(r,s)\quad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(0,T)\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ }r\in(-\infty,T].

(
6.25
) can also be deduced in case (2) using (
6.2
).

By multiplying both inequalities (
6.22
) and
(
6.25
)
by
e^{-2\lambda s}
we get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\Big(e^{-2\lambda s}w^{2}(r,s)\Big)=e^{-2\lambda s}h(r,s)\quad

\displaystyle\text{ in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(-\infty,T)\text{ and every }s\in[0,T],

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Big(e^{-2\lambda s}w^{2}(r,s)\Big)\leq e^{-2\lambda s}\big(f(r,s)-h(r,s)\big)\quad

\displaystyle\text{ in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(0,T)\text{ and a.e.\penalty\ }r\in(-\infty,T].

We fix
t\in[0,T]
and
\varepsilon>0
and we apply the Divergence theorem
in
[
NS06
, Lemma 6.15]
on the two-dimensional strip

Q_{0,t}^{\varepsilon}
as in Figure
1
,

Q_{0,t}^{\varepsilon}:=\{(r,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid 0\leq s\leq t\,,\,s-\varepsilon\leq r\leq s\},

Figure 1.
Strip
Q^{\varepsilon}_{0,t}
corresponding to penalization
about the diagonal
\{r=s\}
.

and we get

\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}e^{-2\lambda t}w^{2}(r,t)\,\mathrm{d}r\leq\int_{-\varepsilon}^{0}w^{2}(r,0)\,\mathrm{d}r+\iint_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{0,t}}e^{-2\lambda s}f(r,s)\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}s.

Using

w(t,t)\leq\int_{r}^{t}|\dot{\mu}^{1}|(u)\,\mathrm{d}u+w(r,t)\leq\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}|\dot{\mu}^{1}|(u)\,\mathrm{d}u+w(r,t)\quad\text{ if }t-\varepsilon\leq r\leq t,

then, for every
\vartheta,\vartheta_{\star}>1
conjugate coefficients (
\vartheta_{\star}=\vartheta/(\vartheta-1)
), we get

(6.26)

w^{2}(t,t)\leq\vartheta w^{2}(r,t)+\vartheta_{\star}\left(\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}|\dot{\mu}^{1}|(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\right)^{2}.

Integrating (
6.26
) w.r.t.
r
in the interval
(t-\varepsilon,t)
, we obtain

(6.27)

e^{-2\lambda t}w^{2}(t,t)\leq\frac{\vartheta}{\varepsilon}\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}e^{-2\lambda t}w^{2}(r,t)\,\mathrm{d}r+\vartheta_{\star}\left(\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}|\dot{\mu}^{1}|(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\right)^{2}\max\{1,e^{2|\lambda|T}\}.

Finally, we have the following inequality

(6.28)

\varepsilon^{-1}\iint_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{0,t}}e^{-2\lambda s}f(r,s)\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}s\leq 2|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu_{0})\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}e^{-2\lambda s}w(0,s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Summing up (
6.27
) and (
6.28
) we obtain

e^{-2\lambda t}w^{2}(t)\leq\vartheta\left(w^{2}(0)+2|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu_{0})\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}e^{-2\lambda s}w(0,s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)+\vartheta_{\star}\left(\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}|\dot{\mu}^{1}|(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\right)^{2}\max\{1,e^{2|\lambda|T}\}.

where we have used the notation
w(s)=w(s,s)
. Taking the limit as

\varepsilon\downarrow 0
and
\vartheta\downarrow 1
, we obtain the
thesis.
∎

Corollary 6.18

(Local Lipschitz estimate)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
and let
\mu:(0,T)\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
,
T\in(0,+\infty]
, be
a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution to (
6.1
).
If at least one of the following two conditions holds

(a)

\mu
is strict and (
EE
) is locally solvable in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,

(b)

\mu
is locally absolutely continuous
and (
4.16
) holds,

then
\mu
is locally Lipschitz and

(6.29)

t\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}|\dot{\mu}|_{+}(t)\quad\text{is decreasing in }(0,T).

Proof.

Since for every
h>0
the curve

t\mapsto\mu_{t+h}
is a
\lambda
-EVI solution,
(
6.21
) yields

\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda(t-s)}W_{2}(\mu_{t+h},\mu_{t})\leq W_{2}(\mu_{s+h},\mu_{s})\quad\text{for every $0<s<t$.}

Dividing by
h
and taking the limsup as
h\downarrow 0
, we
get (
6.29
), which in turn shows the local Lipschitz character
of
\mu
.
∎

6.4.
Global existence and generation of
\lambda
-flows

We collect here a few simple results on the existence of global solutions and the generation of a
\lambda
-flow.
A first result can be deduced from the
global solvability of the Explicit Euler scheme.

Theorem 6.19

(Global existence)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
.
If the Explicit Euler Scheme is globally solvable at
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
then
there exists a unique global
\lambda
-
EVI
solution

\mu\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\rm loc}([0,\infty);\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})})
starting from
\mu_{0}
.

Proof.

We can argue as in the proof of
Theorem
6.11
(a), observing that
the global solvability of (
EE
) allows for
the construction of a limit solution on every interval
[0,T]
,
T>0
.
∎

Let us provide a simple condition ensuring global solvability,
whose proof is deferred to Section
7
.

Proposition 6.20
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
such that for every
R>0
there exist
M=\mathrm{M}(R)>0

and
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\tau}(R)>0

such that

(6.30)

\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),\ \mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu)\leq R,\ 0<\tau\leq\bar{\tau}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\exists\,\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:|\Phi|_{2}\leq\mathrm{M}(R),\ \operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{\tau}_{\sharp}\Phi\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}).

Then the Explicit Euler scheme is globally solvable in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.

Global existence of
\lambda
-EVI solution is also related to
the existence of a
\lambda
-flow.

Definition 6.21
.

We say that the
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

generates a
\lambda
-flow
if
for every
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
there exists a unique

\lambda
-EVI solution
\mu=\mathrm{S}[\mu_{0}]
starting from
\mu_{0}

and the maps
\mu_{0}\mapsto\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]=(\mathrm{S}[\mu_{0}])_{t}

induce a semigroup of Lipschitz transformations

(\mathrm{S}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of
\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
satisfying

(6.31)

W_{2}(\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}],\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{1}])\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\quad\text{for every }t\geq 0.

Theorem 6.22

(Generation of a
\lambda
-flow)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
.
If at least one of the following properties is satisfied:

(a)

the Explicit Euler Scheme is globally solvable
for every
\mu_{0}
in a dense subset of
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
;

(b)

the Explicit Euler Scheme is locally solvable
in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and,
for every
\mu_{0}
in a dense subset of
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
there exists a strict global
\lambda
-
EVI
solution starting from

\mu_{0}
;

(c)

the Explicit Euler Scheme is locally solvable
in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is closed;

(d)

for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
\mu_{1}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

\Gamma_{o}^{0}({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\neq\emptyset

and,
for every
\mu_{0}
in a dense subset of
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
there exists a locally absolutely continuous strict global

\lambda
-
EVI
solution starting from
\mu_{0}
;

(e)

for every

\mu_{0}
in a dense subset of
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
there exists a locally absolutely continuous
solution of (
6.2
) starting from
\mu_{0}
,

then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
generates a
\lambda
-flow.

Proof.

(a) Let
D
be the dense subset of
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
for which
(
EE
) is globally solvable.
For every
\mu_{0}\in D
we define

\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]
,
t\geq 0
, as the value at time
t
of the unique

\lambda
-EVI solution starting from
\mu_{0}
, whose existence
is guaranteed by Theorem
6.19
.

If
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in D
,
T>0
, we can find

\boldsymbol{\tau},L

such that
\mathscr{M}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)

and

\mathscr{M}(\mu_{1},\tau,T,L)
are not empty
for every
\tau\in(0,\boldsymbol{\tau})
.
We can then pass to the limit in the uniform estimate
(
6.12
)
for every choice of
M^{i}_{\tau}\in\mathscr{M}(\mu_{i},\tau,T,L)
,
i=0,1
, obtaining
(
6.31
) for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in D
.

We can then extend the map
\mathrm{S}_{t}
to
\overline{D}=\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

still preserving the same property. Proposition
6.7

shows that for every
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

the continuous curve
t\mapsto\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]
is a

\lambda
-EVI solution starting from
\mu_{0}
.

Finally, if
\mu\in\mathrm{C}([0,T^{\prime});\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})})
is
any
\lambda
-EVI solution starting from
\mu_{0}
,
we can apply (
6.13
) to get

(6.32)

W_{2}(\mu_{t},M^{1}_{\tau}(t))\leq\Big(2W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+C(\boldsymbol{\tau},L,T)\sqrt{\tau}\Big)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}t}\quad\text{for every }t\in[0,T],

for every
T<T^{\prime}
,
\tau<\boldsymbol{\tau}
, where
C(\boldsymbol{\tau},L,T)>0
is a suitable constant.
Passing to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0
in (
6.32
)
we obtain

(6.33)

W_{2}(\mu_{t},\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{1}])\leq 2W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}t}\quad\text{for every }t\in[0,T].

Choosing now a sequence
\mu_{1,n}
in
D
converging to
\mu_{0}

and observing that we can choose arbitrary
T<T^{\prime}
, we eventually get

\mu_{t}=\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]
for every
t\in[0,T^{\prime})
.

(b)
Let
D
be the dense subset of
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
such that
there exists a global strict
\lambda
-EVI solution starting from
D
.
By Theorem
6.15
such a solution is unique
and the corresponding family of solution maps
\mathrm{S}_{t}:D\to\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

satisfy (
6.31
). Arguing as in the previous claim,
we can extend
\mathrm{S}_{t}
to
\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
still preserving (
6.31
)
and the fact that
t\mapsto\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]
is a
\lambda
-EVI solution.

If
\mu
is
\lambda
-EVI solution starting from
\mu_{0}
,
Theorem
6.15
shows that (
6.33
)
holds for every
\mu_{1}\in D
. By approximation we conclude
that
\mu_{t}=\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]
.

(c)
Corollary
6.12
shows that for every
initial datum
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

there exists a global
\lambda
-EVI solution. We can then apply
Claim (b).

(d)
Let
D
be the dense subset of
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
such that
there exists a locally absolutely continuous strict global
\lambda
-EVI solution starting from
D
.
By Theorem
6.16
such a solution is the unique locally absolutely continuous solution starting from
\mu_{0}
and the corresponding family of solution maps
\mathrm{S}_{t}:D\to\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

satisfy (
6.31
). Arguing as in the previous claim (b),
we can extend
\mathrm{S}_{t}
to
\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
still preserving (
6.31
) (again thanks to Theorem
6.16
) and the fact that
t\mapsto\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]
is a
\lambda
-EVI solution.

If
\mu
is a
\lambda
-EVI solution starting from
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
and
(\mu_{0}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset D
is a sequence converging to
\mu_{0}
, we can apply Theorem
6.17
(1) and conclude
that
\mu_{t}=\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]
.

(e)
The proof follows by the same argument of the previous claim,
eventually applying Theorem
6.17
(2).
∎

By Lemma
6.13
we
immediately get the following result.

Corollary 6.23
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is locally bounded
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
with

\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
then for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

there exists a unique global
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
starting from
\mu_{0}
.

We conclude this section by showing a consistency result
with the Hilbertian theory, related to the example
of Section
5.2
.

Corollary 6.24

(Consistency with the theory of contraction
semigroups in Hilbert spaces)
.

Let
F\subset\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}
be a
dissipative maximal subset
generating the
semigroup
(R_{t})_{t\geq 0}

of nonlinear contractions
[
Bré73
, Theorem
3.1]
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be the dissipative
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}:=\{\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})\mid\Phi\text{ is concentrated
on }F\}.

The semigroup

\mu_{0}\mapsto\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]:=(R_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}
,
t\geq 0
,
is the
0
-flow generated by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
in
\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
.

Proof.

Let
D
be the set of
discrete measures
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{j}}

with
x_{j}\in\mathrm{D}(F)
.
Since every
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
is supported
in
\overline{\mathrm{D}(F)}
,
D
is dense in
\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
.
Our thesis follows by applying Theorem
6.22
(e)
if we show that for every

\mu_{0}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{j,0}}\in D

there exists a locally absolutely continuous solution

\mu:[0,\infty)\to D
of (
6.2
) starting from
\mu_{0}
.

It can be directly checked that

\mu_{t}:=(R_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{j,t}},\quad x_{j,t}:=R_{t}(x_{j,0})

satisfies the continuity equation with Wasserstein velocity vector

\boldsymbol{v}_{t}
(defined on the finite support of
\mu_{t}
) satisfying

\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x_{j,t})=\dot{x}_{j,t}=F^{\circ}(x_{j,t}),\quad|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x_{j,t})|\leq|F^{\circ}(x_{j,0})|\quad\text{for every }j=1,\cdots,n,\ \text{and a.e.\penalty\ }t>0,

where
F^{\circ}
is the minimal selection of
F
. It follows that

(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]\quad\text{ for a.e. }t>0,

so that
\mu
is a Lipschitz EVI solution for
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
starting from

\mu_{0}
. We can thus conclude observing that
the map
\mu_{0}\mapsto(R_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}

are contractions in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and
the curve
\mu_{t}=(R_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}

is continuous with values in
\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
.
∎

6.5.
Barycentric property

If we assume that the MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a sequentially closed
subset of
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
with
convex sections, we are able to provide a stronger result showing a particular property satisfied by the solutions of (
6.1
) (see Theorem
6.27
).
This is called
barycentric property
and it is strictly connected with the weaker definition of solution discussed in
[
Pic19
,
Pic18
,
Cam+21
]
.

We first introduce a directional closure of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
along
smooth cylindrical deformations.
We set

\mathrm{exp}^{\varphi}(x):=x+\nabla\varphi(x)\quad\text{for every }\varphi\in\Cyl(\mathsf{X})

and

(6.34)

\displaystyle{\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}[\mu]:={}\Big\{\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\mid{}

\displaystyle\exists\,\varphi\in\Cyl(\mathsf{X}),\ (r_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset[0,+\infty),\ r_{n}\downarrow 0,\ \Phi_{n}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mathrm{exp}^{r_{n}\varphi}_{\sharp}\mu]:

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}\to\Phi\text{ in }\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})\Big\}.

Definition 6.25

(Barycentric property)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a MPVF.
We say that a locally absolutely continuous curve

\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
satisfies the

barycentric property

(resp. the
relaxed
barycentric property
)
if for a.e.
t\in\mathcal{I}
there exists

\Phi_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]

(resp.
\Phi_{t}\in\overline{\operatorname{co}}({\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}[\mu_{t}])
)
s.t.

(6.35)

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x)=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle\nabla\varphi(x),v\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}(x,v)\quad\forall\,\varphi\in\Cyl(\mathsf{X}).

Notice that

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset{\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}\subset\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and
{\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is sequentially closed in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
. Recalling Proposition
4.17
(a)
we also get

\overline{\operatorname{co}}({\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}})\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},

so that the relaxed barycentric property implies
the corresponding property for the extended MPVF
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

Remark 6.26
.

If
\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the property stated in Definition
6.25
coincides with the weak definition of solution to (
6.1
) given in
[
Pic18
]
.

The aim is to prove that the
\lambda
-EVI solution of (
6.1
) enjoys the barycentric property of Definition
6.25
, under suitable mild conditions on
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
. This is
strictly related to the behaviour of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
along the family of smooth deformations
induced by cylindrical functions.
Let us denote by
\mathbf{pr}_{\mu}
the orthogonal projection in

L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})
onto the tangent space
\Tan_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and by
\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}
the barycenter of
\Phi
as in Definition
3.1
.

Theorem 6.27
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a

\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
such that for every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

there exist constants

M,\varepsilon>0

such that

(6.36)

\forall\varphi\in\Cyl(\mathsf{X}):\ \sup_{\mathsf{X}}|\nabla\varphi|\leq\varepsilon\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathrm{exp}^{\varphi}_{\sharp}\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),\ |{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mathrm{exp}^{\varphi}_{\sharp}\mu)<M.

If
\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is a locally absolutely continuous

\lambda
-
EVI
solution of (
6.1
)
with Wasserstein velocity
field

\boldsymbol{v}
satisfying (
2.6
)
for every
t
in the subset
A(\mu)\subset\mathcal{I}
of full
Lebesgue measure, then

(6.37)

\text{for every $t\in A(\mu)$ there exists $\Phi_{t}\in\overline{\operatorname{co}}({\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}})[\mu_{t}]$ such that}\quad\boldsymbol{v}_{t}=\mathbf{pr}_{\mu_{t}}\circ\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{t}}.

In particular,

\mu
satisfies the relaxed barycentric property.

If moreover
{\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and for every
\nu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]
is a convex subset of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
, then

\mu
satisfies (
6.35
).

Proof.

In the following
t
is a fixed element of
A(\mu)
and
M
is the constant associated to the measure
\mu_{t}

in (
6.36
).
For every

\zeta\in\Cyl(\mathsf{X})
there exists
\delta=\delta(\zeta)>0
such that

\nu^{\zeta}:=\mathrm{exp}^{-\delta\zeta}_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\zeta}:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\mathrm{exp}^{-\delta\zeta})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in\Gamma_{o}^{01}({\mu_{t}},{\nu^{\zeta}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is the unique optimal transport plan between
\mu_{t}
and
\nu^{\zeta}
.

Thanks to Theorem
3.11
, the map
s\mapsto W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s},\nu^{\zeta})
is differentiable at
s=t
, moreover by employing also (
6.5b
), it holds

(6.38)

\delta\int_{\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x),\nabla\zeta(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x)=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu^{\zeta})\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\zeta}]_{0+}=\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\,[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\zeta}]_{l,s}.

We can choose a decreasing vanishing sequence
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,1)
, measures
\nu_{k}^{\zeta}:=\mathsf{x}^{s_{k}}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\zeta}

and
\Phi_{k}^{\zeta}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu_{k}^{\zeta}]
such that

\sup_{k}|\Phi_{k}^{\zeta}|_{2}\leq M
and
\Phi_{k}^{\zeta}\to\Phi^{\zeta}

in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
.
Then,
by (
6.13
), we get
\Phi^{\zeta}\in{\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}[\mu_{t}]
with

|\Phi^{\zeta}|_{2}\leq M

and by (
6.38
) and the upper semicontinuity of
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{l}
(see Lemma
3.14
)
we get

(6.39)

\delta\int_{\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x),\nabla\zeta(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x)\leq\left[\Phi^{\zeta},\nu^{\zeta}\right]_{l}=\delta\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle v,\nabla\zeta(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi^{\zeta}(x,v).

Indeed, notice that, by
[
AGS08
, Lemma 5.3.2]
, we have

\Lambda(\Phi^{\zeta},\nu^{\zeta})=\{\Phi^{\zeta}\otimes\nu^{\zeta}\}

with

(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}(\Phi^{\zeta}\otimes\nu^{\zeta})=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\zeta}
.

By means of the identity highlighted in Remark
3.2
, the expression in (
6.39
) can be written as follows

\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t},\nabla\zeta\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mu_{t}}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})}\leq\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi^{\zeta}},\nabla\zeta\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mu_{t}}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})}=\langle\mathbf{pr}_{\mu_{t}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi^{\zeta}}),\nabla\zeta\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mu_{t}}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})}

so that

\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t},\nabla\zeta\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mu_{t}}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})}\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{b}\in K}\,\langle\boldsymbol{b},\nabla\zeta\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mu_{t}}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})}\quad\text{for all }\,\zeta\in\Cyl(\mathsf{X})

where

(6.40)

K:=\left\{\mathbf{pr}_{\mu_{t}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi})\,:\,\Phi\in{\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}[\mu_{t}],\,|\Phi|_{2}\leq M\right\}\subset\Tan_{\mu_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

Applying Lemma
C.1
in

\Tan_{\mu_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\subset L^{2}_{\mu_{t}}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})

we obtain that
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\in\overline{\operatorname{co}}(K)
.
In order to obtain (
6.37
) it is sufficient to prove that

\boldsymbol{v}_{t}
is the
L^{2}
-projection of the barycenter of an element of

\overline{\operatorname{co}}({\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}[\mu_{t}])
.

Notice that an element
\boldsymbol{v}\in\Tan_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

coincides with
\mathbf{pr}_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi})
for
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu)
if and only
if

(6.41)

\int\langle\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\zeta\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\int\langle v,\nabla\zeta\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi(x,v)\quad\text{for every }\zeta\in\mathrm{Cyl}(\mathsf{X}).

It is easy to check that
any element
\boldsymbol{v}\in\operatorname{co}(K)
can be represented as

\mathbf{pr}_{\mu_{t}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi})
(and thus as in (
6.41
)) for some

\Phi\in\operatorname{co}({\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}[\mu_{t}])
.
If
\boldsymbol{v}\in\overline{\operatorname{co}}(K)
we can find
a sequence
\Phi_{n}\in\operatorname{co}({\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}[\mu_{t}])
such that

|\Phi_{n}|_{2}\leq M
and

\boldsymbol{v}_{n}=\mathbf{pr}_{\mu_{t}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{n}})\to\boldsymbol{v}
in
L^{2}_{\mu_{t}}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})
.
Since the sequence
(\Phi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is relatively compact in

\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
by Proposition
2.15
(2),
we can extract a (not relabeled)
subsequence converging to
a limit
\Phi
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
, as
n\to+\infty
.
By definition
\Phi\in\overline{\operatorname{co}}({\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}[\mu_{t}])
with

|\Phi|_{2}\leq M
.
We can eventually pass to the limit in (
6.41
)
written for
\boldsymbol{v}_{n}
and
\Phi_{n}

thanks to
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
convergence, obtaining
the corresponding identity for
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\Phi
in the limit.

Finally, being
\mu
locally absolutely continuous, it satisfies the continuity equation driven by
\boldsymbol{v}
in the sense of distributions (see Theorem
2.10
), so that

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\zeta(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x)=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\langle\nabla\zeta(x),\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x)=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle\nabla\zeta(x),v\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}(x,v)\quad\forall\zeta\in\text{Cyl}(\mathsf{X}).\qed

Remark 6.28
.

We notice that it is always possible to
estimate the
value of
M
in (
6.40
) by
|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2\star}(\mu_{t})
.

Remark 6.29
.

Using a standard approximation argument (see for example the proof of Lemma 5.1.12(f) in
[
AGS08
]
) it is possible to show that actually the barycentric property (
6.35
) holds for every
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{1,1}(\mathsf{X};\mathbb{R})
(indeed, in this case,
\nabla\varphi\in\Tan_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
).

As a complement to the studies investigated in this section, we prove
the converse characterization of Theorem
6.27
in the
particular case of
regular measures

or
regular vector fields
.
We refer to
[
AGS08
, Definitions 6.2.1, 6.2.2]
for the definition of

\mathcal{P}_{2}^{r}(\mathsf{X})
, that is the space of regular measures on
\mathsf{X}
.
When
\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
has finite dimension,
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{r}(\mathsf{X})

is just the subset of measures in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
which are absolutely
continuous
w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure
\mathcal{L}^{d}
.

Theorem 6.30
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
. Let
\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

be a locally absolutely continuous curve satisfying the relaxed
barycentric property of Definition
6.25
.
If for a.e.
t\in\mathcal{I}
at least one of the following properties holds:

(1)

\mu_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}^{r}(\mathsf{X})
,

(2)

{\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}[\mu_{t}]

contains a unique element
\Phi_{t}
concentrated
on a map, i.e.
\Phi_{t}=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{t}})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}

then
\mu
is
\lambda
-
EVI
solution of (
6.1
).

Proof.

Take
\varphi\in\Cyl(\mathsf{X})
and observe that, since
\mu
has the relaxed barycentric
property, then
for a.e.
t\in\mathcal{I}
(recall
Theorem
3.11
)
there exists
\Phi_{t}\in\overline{\operatorname{co}}({\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}[\mu_{t}])
such that

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x)=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle\nabla\varphi(x),v\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\langle\nabla\varphi,\mathbf{pr}_{\mu_{t}}\circ\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{t}}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t},\nabla\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t},

hence
\mu
solves the continuity equation

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\text{div}(\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\mu_{t})=0
, with

\boldsymbol{v}_{t}=\mathbf{pr}_{\mu_{t}}\circ\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{t}}\in\Tan_{\mu_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
.
By Theorem
3.11
, we also know that

(6.42)

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)=\int_{\mathsf{X}^{2}}\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x_{0}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{t}(x_{0},x_{1}),\quad t\in A(\mu,\nu),\ \boldsymbol{\gamma}_{t}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t},\nu),\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

Possibly disregarding a Lebesgue negligible set, we can
decompose the set
A(\mu,\nu)
in the union

A_{1}\cup A_{2}
, where
A_{1},A_{2}
correspond to
the times
t
for which the properties (1) and (2) hold.

If
t\in A_{1}
and
\nu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, then by
[
AGS08
, Theorem
6.2.10]
, since
\mu_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}^{r}(\mathsf{X})
, there exists a unique

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{t}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t},\nu)
and

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{t}=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{r}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}
for some map
\boldsymbol{r}_{t}
s.t.
\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}}-\boldsymbol{r}_{t}\in\Tan_{\mu_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\subset L^{2}_{\mu_{t}}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})

(recall
[
AGS08
, Proposition 8.5.2]
), so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathsf{X}^{2}}\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x_{0}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{t}(x_{0},x_{1})=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x_{0}),x_{0}-\boldsymbol{r}_{t}(x_{0})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x_{0})

(6.43)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{t}},x_{0}-\boldsymbol{r}_{t}(x_{0})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x_{0})=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle v,x-\boldsymbol{r}_{t}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}(x,v)=\left[\Phi_{t},\nu\right]_{r},

where we also applied Theorem
3.9

and Remark
3.18
,
recalling that in this case
\Lambda(\Phi_{t},\nu)
is a singleton.

If
t\in A_{2}
we can select
the optimal plan
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{t}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t},\nu)

along which

\left[\Phi_{t},\nu\right]_{r}=[\Phi_{t},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{t}]_{r,0}=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{t}}(x_{0}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{t}(x_{0},x_{1}).

If
\boldsymbol{r}_{t}
is the barycenter of
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{t}

with respect to its first marginal
\mu_{t}
,
recalling that
\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}}-\boldsymbol{r}_{t}\in\Tan_{\mu_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

(see also the proof of
[
AGS08
, Thm. 12.4.4]
) we also get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathsf{X}^{2}}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x_{0}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{t}(x_{0},x_{1})=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x_{0}),x_{0}-\boldsymbol{r}_{t}(x_{0})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x_{0})

(6.44)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{t}}(x_{0}),x_{0}-\boldsymbol{r}_{t}(x_{0})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x_{0})=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{t}}(x_{0}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{t}(x_{0},x_{1})=\left[\Phi_{t},\nu\right]_{r}

where we still applied Theorem
3.9

and Remark
3.18
.

Combining (
6.42
) with (
6.43
) and (
6.44
)
we eventually get

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)

\displaystyle=\left[\Phi_{t},\nu\right]_{r}\leq-\left[\Psi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}+\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu),\quad\forall\,\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu],

by definition of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and the fact that
\overline{\operatorname{co}}(\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}})[\mu_{t}]\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]
.
∎

Thanks to Theorem
6.30
,
we can apply to barycentric solutions
the uniqueness and approximation results
of the previous Sections.
We conclude this section with a general result on the existence of a
\lambda
-flow
for
\lambda
-dissipative MPVFs, which is the natural refinement of Proposition

6.14

Theorem 6.31

(Generation of
\lambda
-flow)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
such that
\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathsf{X})\subset\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

and for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathsf{X})
there exist
\varrho>0
and
L>0

such that

(6.45)

\supp(\mu)\subset\supp(\mu_{0})+\mathrm{B}_{\mathsf{X}}(\varrho)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\exists\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:\supp(\mathsf{v}_{\sharp}\Phi)\subset\mathrm{B}_{\mathsf{X}}(L).

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{b}:={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\cap\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
. If there exists
a\geq 0
such that for every
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{b}

(6.46)

\supp(\Phi)\subset\Big\{(x,v)\in\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}:\langle v,x\rangle\leq a(1+|x|^{2})\Big\},

then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
generates a
\lambda
-flow.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{b}
generates a
\lambda
-flow.
Applying Proposition
6.14
to the MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{b}
,
we know that for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{b})

there exists a unique maximal strict
\lambda
-EVI solution

\mu\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\rm loc}([0,T);\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathsf{X}))
driven by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{b}
and satisfying (
6.16
).
We argue by contradiction, and we assume that
T<+\infty
.
Notice that by (
6.45
)
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
satisfies (
6.36
),
so that
\mu
is a relaxed barycentric solution for
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{b}
. Since

\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathsf{X})
, we know that
\supp(\mu_{0})\subset\mathrm{B}_{\mathsf{X}}(r_{0})
for some

r_{0}>1
.

It is easy to check that (
6.46
) holds
also for every
\Phi\in\overline{\operatorname{co}}({\overline{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}_{b})
.
Moreover, setting
b:=2a
, condition (
6.46
) yields

(6.47)

\langle v,x\rangle\leq b|x|^{2}\quad\text{for every}\quad(x,v)\in\supp\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{b},\ |x|\geq 1.

Let
\phi(r):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be any smooth increasing function
such that
\phi(r)=0
if
r\leq r_{0}
and
\phi(r)=1
if
r\geq r_{0}+1
,
and
let
\varphi(t,x):=\phi(|x|\mathrm{e}^{-bt})
.
Clearly
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{1,1}(\mathsf{X}\times[0,+\infty))
,
with
\nabla\varphi(t,x)=\frac{x}{|x|}\phi^{\prime}(|x|\mathrm{e}^{-bt})\mathrm{e}^{-bt}

if
x\neq 0
,
\nabla\varphi(t,0)=0
,
and
\partial_{t}\varphi(t,x)=-b\phi^{\prime}(|x|\mathrm{e}^{-bt})|x|\mathrm{e}^{-bt}.

We thus have for a.e.
t\in[0,T)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-bt}\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\Big(-b\phi^{\prime}(|x|\mathrm{e}^{-bt})|x|+\langle v,x\rangle|x|^{-1}\phi^{\prime}(|x|\mathrm{e}^{-bt})\Big)\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}(v,x)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{-bt}\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\Big(-b\phi^{\prime}(|x|\mathrm{e}^{-bt})|x|+b|x|\phi^{\prime}(|x|\mathrm{e}^{-bt})\Big)\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}(v,x)=0

where in the last inequality we used (
6.47
) and
the fact that the integrand vanishes if
|x|\leq 1
.
We get

\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}=0\quad\text{in }[0,T);

this implies that
\supp(\mu_{t})\subset\mathrm{B}_{\mathsf{X}}((r_{0}+1)\mathrm{e}^{bt})

so that the limit measure
\mu_{T}
belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathsf{X})
as well, leading to a contradiction with (
6.16
) for
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{b}
.

We deduce that
\mu
is a global strict
\lambda
-EVI solution for
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{b}
. We can then apply
Theorem
6.22
(b) to
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{b}
.
∎

6.6.
A few borderline examples

We conclude this section with a few examples which reveal
the importance of some of the technical tools we developed so far.
First of all we exhibit an example of dissipative MPVF generating a

0
-flow,
for which
solutions starting from initial data are merely continuous
(in particular the nice regularizing effect of gradient flows does not
hold
for general dissipative evolutions).
This clarifies the interest in a definition of continuous, not necessarily absolutely continuous, solution.

Example 6.32

(Lifting of dissipative evolutions and lack of
regularizing effect)
.

Let us consider the situation of Corollary
6.24
,
choosing the Hilbert space
\mathsf{X}=\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
.
Following
[
Rul96
, Example 3]

we can easily find a maximal linear dissipative operator

A:\mathrm{D}(A)\subset\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})

whose semigroup
does not provide a regularizing effect.

The domain of
A
is

\mathrm{D}(A):=\{x\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}):\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}|x_{k}|^{2}<\infty\}

and
A
is defined as

A(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{2k-1},x_{2k},\dots)=(-x_{2},x_{1},\dots,-kx_{2k},kx_{2k-1},\dots),\quad x\in\mathrm{D}(A),

so that there is no regularizing effect for the semigroup
(R_{t})_{t\geq 0}
generated by (the graph of)
A
:
evolutions starting outside the domain
\mathrm{D}(A)
stay
outside the domain and do not give raise to locally Lipschitz
or a.e. differentiable curves.
Corollary
6.24

shows that the
0
-flow
(S_{t})_{t\geq 0}
generated
by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)

is given by

S_{t}[\mu_{0}]=(R_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}\quad\text{ for every }\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

so that there is the same lack of regularizing effect on probability
measures.

In the next example we show that a constant MPVF generates a barycentric solution.

Example 6.33

(Constant PVF and barycentric evolutions)
.

Given
\theta\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, we consider the constant PVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:=\mu\otimes\theta.

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is dissipative: in fact,
if
\Phi_{i}=\mu_{i}\otimes\theta
,
i=0,1
,

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, and

\boldsymbol{r}:\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}\times X\to\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}
is defined by

\boldsymbol{r}(x_{0},x_{1},v):=(x_{0},v;x_{1},v)
,
then

\Theta=\boldsymbol{r}_{\sharp}(\boldsymbol{\mu}\otimes\theta)\in\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})

so that (
3.16
) yields

\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{r}\leq\int\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v-v\rangle\,\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{\mu}\otimes\theta)(x_{0},x_{1},v)=0.

Applying Proposition
6.20
and Theorem

6.19

we immediately see that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
generates a
0
-flow
(\mathrm{S}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, obtained as a
limit
of the Explicit Euler scheme.
It is also straightforward to notice that we can apply Theorem

6.27
to
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
so that for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
the unique EVI solution
\mu_{t}=\mathrm{S}_{t}\mu_{0}

satisfies the continuity equation

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{b}\mu_{t})=0,\quad\boldsymbol{b}=\int_{\mathsf{X}}x\,\mathrm{d}\theta(x).

Since
\boldsymbol{b}
is constant, we deduce that
\mathrm{S}_{t}

acts as a translation with constant velocity
\boldsymbol{b}
, i.e.

\mu_{t}=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}}+t\boldsymbol{b})_{\sharp}\mu_{0},

so that
\mathrm{S}_{t}
coincides with the semigroup generated
by the PVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}[\mu]:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{b})_{\sharp}\mu
.

We conclude this section with a
1
-dimensional example of a curve which satisfies the barycentric property but it is not an EVI solution.

Example 6.34
.

Let
\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{R}
.
It is well known (see e.g.
[
NS09
]
)
that
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is isometric to
the closed convex subset
\mathcal{K}\subset L^{2}(0,1)
of the (essentially) increasing maps
and the
isometry
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{K}
maps
each measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

into the
pseudo inverse of its cumulative distribution function.

It follows that for every
\bar{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

the functional
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

\mathcal{F}(\mu):=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\bar{\nu})

is
1
-convex, since it satisfies
\mathcal{F}(\mu)=\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{J}(\mu))

where

\mathcal{G}:L^{2}(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as

\mathcal{G}(u):=\frac{1}{2}\|u-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\nu})\|^{2}\quad\text{for
every }u\in L^{2}(0,1).

Thus
\mathcal{F}
generates a gradient flow
(\mathrm{S}_{t})_{t\geq 0}

which is a semigroup of contractions in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
;
for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]
is the unique
(-1)
-EVI solution for the
MPVF
-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}
starting from
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
(see Proposition
6.5
).
Since the notion of gradient flow is purely metric, the gradient flow of
\mathcal{G}
starting from
\mathcal{J}(\mu_{0})
is just the image through
\mathcal{J}
of the gradient flow of
\mathcal{F}
starting from
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
. It is easy to check that

u(t):=\mathrm{e}^{-t}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{0})+(1-\mathrm{e}^{-t})\mathcal{J}(\bar{\nu})

is the gradient flow of
\mathcal{G}
starting from
u_{0}=\mathcal{J}(\mu_{0})
. Note
that
u(t)
is the
L^{2}(0,1)
geodesic from
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\nu})
to

\mathcal{J}(\mu_{0})
evaluated at the rescaled time
e^{-t}
, so that

\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]
must coincide with the evaluation at time

\mathrm{e}^{-t}
of the (unique) geodesic connecting
\bar{\nu}
to
\mu_{0}
i.e.

\mathrm{S}_{t}[\mu_{0}]=\mathsf{x}^{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\gamma},\quad s=\mathrm{e}^{-t}\in(0,1],

where
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\nu},\mu_{0})
.

Let us now consider
the particular case
\bar{\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{-a}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{a}
, where
a>0
is a fixed parameter and
\mu_{0}=\delta_{0}
. It is straightforward to see that

\mu_{t}=\mathrm{S}_{t}[\delta_{0}]=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{a(1-\mathrm{e}^{-t})}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{a(\mathrm{e}^{-t}-1)},\quad t\geq 0

so that

(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{((1-e^{-t})a,e^{-t}a)}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{((e^{-t}-1)a,-e^{-t}a)}\in-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}(\mu_{t}),\quad\text{ a.e. }t>0,

where
\boldsymbol{v}
is the Wasserstein velocity field of
\mu_{t}
.
On the other hand,
[
AGS08
, Lemma 10.3.8]
shows that

\delta_{0}\otimes\left(\frac{1}{2}\delta_{-a}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{a}\right)\in-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}(\delta_{0})

so that the constant curve
\bar{\mu}_{t}:=\delta_{0}
for
t\geq 0

has the barycentric property for the MPVF
-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}
but it is not a EVI solution for
-\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathcal{F}
,
being different from
\mu_{t}=\mathrm{S}_{t}[\delta_{0}]
.

7.
Explicit Euler Scheme

In this section, we collect all
the main estimates concerning the
Explicit Euler scheme (
EE
).

7.1.
The Explicit Euler Scheme: preliminary estimates

Our first step is to prove simple a priori estimates and a discrete
version of (

λ

-EVI
) as a consequence of Proposition

3.4
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Every solution

(M_{\tau},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau})\in\mathscr{E}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)
of (
EE
) satisfies

(7.1)

W_{2}(M_{\tau}(t),\mu_{0})\leq Lt,\quad|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(t)|_{2}\leq L\quad\text{for every $t\in[0,T]$,}

(7.2)

W_{2}(M_{\tau}(t),M_{\tau}(s))\leq L|t-s|\quad\text{for every }s,t\in[0,T],

(IEVI)

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(M_{\tau}(t),\nu)\leq\left[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(t),\nu\right]_{r}+\tau|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(t)|_{2}^{2}\leq\left[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(t),\nu\right]_{r}+\tau L^{2}\quad\text{in $[0,T]$, }\forall\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),

with possibly countable exceptions.
In particular

(7.3)

\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(M_{\tau}^{n+1},\nu)-\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(M_{\tau}^{n},\nu)\leq\tau\left[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n},\nu\right]_{r}+\frac{1}{2}{\tau^{2}}L^{2}\quad\text{for every }0\leq n<{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)},\forall\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

Proof.

The second inequality of (
7.1
) is a trivial consequence of the definition of

\mathscr{E}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)
, the first inequality is a particular case of (
7.2
).
The estimate (
7.2
) is immediate if

n\tau\leq s<t\leq(n+1)\tau
since

\displaystyle W_{2}(M_{\tau}(s),M_{\tau}(t))

\displaystyle=W_{2}((\operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{s-n\tau})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n},(\operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{t-n\tau})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n})\leq\sqrt{\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}|(t-s)v)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n}}

\displaystyle=(t-s)\sqrt{\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}|v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n}}\leq(t-s)L.

This implies that the metric velocity of
M_{\tau}
is bounded by
L

in
[0,T]

and therefore
M_{\tau}
is
L
-Lipschitz.

Let us recall
that
for every
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

the function

g(t):=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{t}_{\sharp}\Phi,\nu)

satisfies

(7.4)

t\mapsto g(t)-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}|\Phi|_{2}^{2}\text{ is concave},\quad g^{\prime}_{r}(0)=\left[\Phi,\nu\right]_{r},\quad g^{\prime}(t)\leq\left[\Phi,\nu\right]_{r}+t|\Phi|_{2}^{2}\quad t\geq 0,

by Definition
3.5
and Proposition
3.4
.
In particular, the concavity yields the differentiability of
g
with at most countable exceptions.
Thus, taking any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq n<{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)}
,

t\in[n\tau,(n+1)\tau)
and
\Phi={\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n}
so that
\operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{t}_{\sharp}\Phi=M_{\tau}(t)
,
(
7.4
) yields
(IEVI)
.
(
7.3
) follows by integration in each interval
[n\tau,(n+1)\tau]
.
∎

In the following, we prove a uniform bound on curves
M_{\tau}\in\mathscr{M}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)

which is useful to prove global solvability of the Explicit Euler scheme,
as stated in Proposition
6.20
.
We will use the following discrete Gronwall estimate: if
a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of positive real numbers satisfies

x_{n+1}-x_{n}\leq\tau y+\tau\alpha x_{n},\quad 1\leq n\leq N,\,\alpha\geq 0,\,y\geq 0,\,\tau>0,

then

(7.5)

x_{n}\leq(x_{0}+\tau ny)\mathrm{e}^{\alpha n\tau}\quad 0\leq n\leq N+1.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
such that for every
R>0
there exist
M=\mathrm{M}(R)>0

and
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\tau}(R)>0

such that

(7.6)

\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),\ \mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu)\leq R,\ 0<\tau\leq\bar{\tau}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\exists\,\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:|\Phi|_{2}\leq\mathrm{M}(R),\ \operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{\tau}_{\sharp}\Phi\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),

then the Explicit Euler scheme is globally solvable in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.
More precisely,
if for a given
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

with
\Psi_{0}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{0}]
,
\mathsf{m}_{0}:=\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu_{0}),
and
we set

(7.7)

R:=\mathsf{m}_{0}+\Big(|\Psi_{0}|_{2}+1\Big)\sqrt{2T}\mathrm{e}^{(1+2\lambda_{+})T},\quad L:=\mathrm{M}(R),\quad\boldsymbol{\tau}=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\land\bar{\tau}(R)\wedge T,

then
for every
\tau\in(0,\boldsymbol{\tau}]
the set

\mathscr{E}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)
is not empty.

Proof.

We want to prove by induction that
for every integer
N\leq{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)}
,
(
EE
) has a solution up to the index
N
satisfying the
upper bound

(7.8)

\mathsf{m}_{2}(M^{N}_{\tau})\leq R,

corresponding to the constants
R,L
given by (
7.7
).
For
N=0
the statement is trivially satisfied.
Assuming that

0\leq N<{\mathrm{N}(T,\tau)}
and elements
(M^{n}_{\tau},{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{n}_{\tau})
,

0\leq n<N
,
M^{N}_{\tau}
,
are given satisfying (
EE
) and (
7.8
),
we want to show that we can perform a further step of the Euler Scheme
so that (
EE
) is solvable up to the index
N+1
and

\mathsf{m}_{2}(M^{N+1}_{\tau})\leq R
.

Notice that by the induction hypothesis, for
n=0,\dots,N-1
, we have

|{\boldsymbol{F}}^{n}_{\tau}|_{2}\leq L
; since

\mathsf{m}_{2}(M^{N}_{\tau})\leq R
, by (
7.6
)
we can select
{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}_{\tau}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[M^{N}_{\tau}]

with
|{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}_{\tau}|_{2}\leq L
such that
M^{N+1}_{\tau}=\operatorname{\mathsf{exp}}^{\tau}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}_{\tau}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.
Using (
7.3
) with
\nu=\mu_{0}
,
the
\lambda
-dissipativity with
\Psi_{0}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{0}]

\left[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{n},\mu_{0}\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(M_{\tau}^{n},\mu_{0})-\left[\Psi_{0},M_{\tau}^{n}\right]_{r},

and the bound

-\left[\Psi_{0},M_{\tau}^{n}\right]_{r}\leq\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(M_{\tau}^{n},\mu_{0})+\frac{1}{2}|\Psi_{0}|_{2}^{2},

we end up with

\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(M_{\tau}^{n+1},\mu_{0})-\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(M_{\tau}^{n},\mu_{0})\leq\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}L^{2}+\tau\left(\frac{1}{2}+\lambda_{+}\right)\,W_{2}^{2}(M_{\tau}^{n},\mu_{0})+\frac{\tau}{2}|\Psi_{0}|_{2}^{2},

for every
n\leq N
.
Using the Gronwall estimate (
7.5
)
we get

\displaystyle W_{2}(M_{\tau}^{n},\mu_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{T+\tau}\Big(|\Psi_{0}|_{2}+\sqrt{\tau}L\Big)\mathrm{e}^{(\frac{1}{2}+\lambda_{+})\,(T+\tau)}\leq\sqrt{2T}\Big(|\Psi_{0}|_{2}+1\Big)\mathrm{e}^{(1+2\lambda_{+})T}

for every
n\leq N+1
, so that

\mathsf{m}_{2}(M^{N+1}_{\tau})\leq\mathsf{m}_{0}+\sqrt{2T}\Big(|\Psi_{0}|_{2}+1\Big)\mathrm{e}^{(1+2\lambda_{+})T}\leq R.\qed

We conclude this section by proving the stability estimate (
6.12
) of Theorem

6.9
.
We introduce the notation

I_{\kappa}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{\kappa r}\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{1}{\kappa}(\mathrm{e}^{\kappa t}-1)\quad\text{if }\kappa\neq 0;\quad I_{0}(t):=t.

Notice that for every
t\geq 0

(7.9)

I_{\kappa}(t)\leq t\mathrm{e}^{\kappa t}\quad\text{if }\kappa\geq 0;\qquad

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
M_{\tau}\in\mathscr{M}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,L)
and

M_{\tau}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{M}(\mu_{0}^{\prime},\tau,T,L)
.
If
\lambda_{+}\tau\leq 2
then

W_{2}(M_{\tau}(t),M_{\tau}^{\prime}(t))\leq W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{0}^{\prime})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}+8L\sqrt{t\tau}\Big(1+|\lambda|\sqrt{t\tau}\Big)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}t}

for every
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

Let us set

w(t):=W_{2}(M_{\tau}(t),M_{\tau}^{\prime}(t))
.
Since by Proposition
3.4
(2),
in every interval
[n\tau,(n+1)\tau]
the function
t\mapsto w^{2}(t)-4L^{2}(t-n\tau)^{2}
is concave, with

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}w^{2}(t)\bigg|_{t=n\tau+}=2\left[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(t),{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}^{\prime}(t)\right]_{r}\leq 2\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau}(t),\bar{M}_{\tau}^{\prime}(t)),

we obtain

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}w^{2}(t)\leq 2\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau}(t),\bar{M}_{\tau}^{\prime}(t))+8L^{2}\tau\quad t\in[0,T],

with possibly countable exceptions.
Using the identity
a^{2}-b^{2}=2b(a-b)+|a-b|^{2}

with
a=W_{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau}(t),\bar{M}_{\tau}^{\prime}(t))
and

b=W_{2}(M_{\tau}(t),M_{\tau}^{\prime}(t))
and observing that

|a-b|\leq W_{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau}(t),M_{\tau}(t))+W_{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau}^{\prime}(t),M_{\tau}^{\prime}(t))\leq 2L\tau
,
we eventually get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}w^{2}(t)

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda w^{2}(t)+8L^{2}\tau+8|\lambda|L\tau w(t)+\lambda_{+}8L^{2}\tau^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda w^{2}(t)+8|\lambda|L\tau w(t)+24L^{2}\tau,

since
\lambda_{+}\tau\leq 2
by assumption.
The Gronwall lemma
[
AGS08
, Lemma 4.1.8]
and (
7.9
) yield

\displaystyle w(t)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(w^{2}(0)\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda t}+24L^{2}\tau\mathrm{I}_{2\lambda}(t)\Big)^{1/2}+8|\lambda|L\tau\mathrm{I}_{\lambda}(t)

\displaystyle\leq w(0)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}+8L\sqrt{t\tau}\Big(1+|\lambda|\sqrt{t\tau}\Big)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}t}.\qed

7.2.
Error estimates for the Explicit Euler scheme

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a

\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
.
If
M_{\tau}\in\mathscr{M}(M^{0}_{\tau},\tau,T,L)
,
M_{\eta}\in\mathscr{M}(M^{0}_{\eta},\eta,T,L)

with
\lambda\sqrt{T(\tau+\eta)}\leq 1
,
then for every
\vartheta>1

there exists a constant
C(\vartheta)
such that

W_{2}(M_{\tau}(t),M_{\eta}(t))\leq\Big(\sqrt{\vartheta}W_{2}(M^{0}_{\tau},M^{0}_{\eta})+C(\vartheta)L\sqrt{(\tau+\eta)(t+\tau+\eta)}\Big)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}\,t}

for every
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

We argue as in the proof of Theorem
6.17

with the aim to gain a convenient order of convergence.
Since
\lambda
-dissipativity implies
\lambda^{\prime}
-dissipativity for
\lambda^{\prime}\geq\lambda
,
it is not restrictive to assume

\lambda>0
. We set
\sigma:=\tau+\eta
.
We will extensively use the a priori bounds (
7.1
) and (
7.2
);
in particular,

W_{2}(M_{\tau}(t),\bar{M}_{\tau}(t))\leq L\tau,\quad W_{2}(M_{\eta}(t),\bar{M}_{\eta}(t))\leq L\eta.

We will also extend
M_{\tau}
and
\bar{M}_{\tau}
for negative times by setting

(7.10)

M_{\tau}(t)=\bar{M}_{\tau}(t)=M^{0}_{\tau},\quad{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\tau}(t)=M^{0}_{\tau}\otimes\delta_{0}\quad\text{if }t<0.

The proof is divided into several steps.

1. Doubling variables.

We fix a final time
t\in[0,T]
and
two variables
r,s\in[0,t]
together with
the functions

(7.11)

\displaystyle w(r,s):={}

\displaystyle W_{2}(M_{\tau}(r),M_{\eta}(s)),\quad

\displaystyle w_{\tau}(r,s):={}

\displaystyle W_{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau}(r),M_{\eta}(s)),

\displaystyle w_{\eta}(r,s):={}

\displaystyle W_{2}(M_{\tau}(r),\bar{M}_{\eta}(s)),\quad

\displaystyle w_{\tau,\eta}(r,s):={}

\displaystyle W_{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau}(r),\bar{M}_{\eta}(s)),

observing that

(7.12)

|w-w_{\tau}|\lor|w_{\eta}-w_{\tau,\eta}|\leq L\tau,\quad|w-w_{\eta}|\lor|w_{\tau}-w_{\tau,\eta}|\leq L\eta.

By Proposition
7.1
, we can write
(IEVI)
both for
M_{\tau}
and
M_{\eta}
and we obtain

(
\text{IEVI}_{\tau}
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(M_{\tau}(r),\nu_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\tau|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(r)|_{2}^{2}+\left[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(r),\nu_{1}\right]_{r}\quad\forall\nu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(M_{\eta}(s),\nu_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\eta|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\eta}(s)|_{2}^{2}+\left[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\eta}(s),\nu_{2}\right]_{r}

(
\text{IEVI}_{\eta}
)

\displaystyle\leq\eta|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\eta}(s)|_{2}^{2}+\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\bar{M}_{\eta}(s),\nu_{2})-\left[\Phi,\bar{M}_{\eta}(s)\right]_{r},\quad\forall\nu_{2}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),\,\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu_{2}].

Apart from possible countable exceptions, (

IEVI
τ

) holds for
r\in(-\infty,t]
and
(

IEVI
η

) for
s\in[0,t]
.
Taking

\nu_{1}=\bar{M}_{\eta}(s)
,

\nu_{2}=\bar{M}_{\tau}(r)
,

\Phi={\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(r\lor 0)\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\bar{M}_{\tau}(r)]
,
summing the two inequalities

(\text{IEVI}_{\tau,\eta})
, setting

f(r,s):=\begin{cases}2LW_{2}(\bar{M}_{\eta}(s),M_{\tau}(0))=2Lw_{\eta}(0,s)&\text{if }r<0,\\
0&\text{if }r\geq 0,\end{cases}

using (
7.1
)
and the
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
, we obtain

\frac{\partial}{\partial r}w_{\eta}^{2}(r,s)+\frac{\partial}{\partial s}w_{\tau}^{2}(r,s)\leq 2\lambda w_{\tau,\eta}^{2}(r,s)+2L^{2}\sigma+f(r,s)

in
(-\infty,t]\times[0,t]

(see also
[
NS06
, Lemma 6.15]
).
By multiplying both sides by
e^{-2\lambda s}
, we have

(7.13)

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}w_{\eta}^{2}+\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}w_{\tau}^{2}\leq\Big(2\lambda\left(w_{\tau,\eta}^{2}-w_{\tau}^{2}\right)+f+2L^{2}\sigma\Big)\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}.\end{split}

Using (
7.12
), the inequality

w_{\tau,\eta}+w_{\tau}=w_{\tau,\eta}-w_{\tau}+2(w_{\tau}-w)+2w\leq 2L\sigma+2w,\quad|w(r,s)-w(s,s)|\leq L|r-s|

and the elementary inequality

a^{2}-b^{2}\leq|a-b||a+b|,

we get

2\big(w_{\tau,\eta}^{2}(r,s)-w_{\tau}^{2}(r,s)\big)\leq R,\quad R:=4L^{2}\sigma(\sigma+|r-s|)+4L\sigma w(s,s)\quad\text{if }r,s\leq t.

Thus (
7.13
) becomes

(7.14)

\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}w_{\eta}^{2}+\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}w_{\tau}^{2}\leq Z,\quad Z:=\Big(R\lambda+f+2L^{2}\sigma\Big)\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}.

2. Penalization.

We fix any
\varepsilon>0

and apply the Divergence Theorem to the inequality (
7.14
) in the two-dimensional strip
Q^{\varepsilon}_{0,t}
as in Figure
1
and we get

(7.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}&\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda t}w_{\tau}^{2}(r,t)\,\mathrm{d}r\leq\int_{-\varepsilon}^{0}w^{2}_{\tau}(r,0)\,\mathrm{d}r+\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}\left(w_{\tau}^{2}(s,s)-w_{\eta}^{2}(s,s)\right)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}\left(w_{\eta}^{2}(s-\varepsilon,s)-w_{\tau}^{2}(s-\varepsilon,s)\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&+\iint_{Q_{0,t}^{\varepsilon}}Z\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

3. Estimates of the RHS.

We want to estimate the integrals
(say
I_{0},I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})

of the right hand side of (
7.15
)
in terms of

w(s):=w(s,s)\quad\text{and}\quad W(t):=\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}w(s).

We easily get

I_{0}=\int_{-\varepsilon}^{0}w_{\tau}^{2}(r,0)\,\mathrm{d}r=\varepsilon w^{2}(0).

(
7.12
) yields

\displaystyle|w_{\tau}(s,s)-w_{\eta}(s,s)|\leq L(\tau+\eta)=L\sigma

and

|w^{2}_{\tau}(s,s)-w^{2}_{\eta}(s,s)|\leq L\sigma\Big(L\sigma+2w(s)\Big);

after an integration,

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq{L^{2}\sigma^{2}t}+2L\sigma\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda s}w(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq L^{2}\sigma^{2}t+2L\sigma tW(t).

Performing the same computations for the third integral term at the RHS of (
7.15
) we end up with
