\begin{split}I_{2}&=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda s}\left(w_{\eta}^{2}(s-\varepsilon,s)-w_{\tau}^{2}(s-\varepsilon,s)\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq{L^{2}t\sigma^{2}}+2L\sigma\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda s}w(s-\varepsilon,s)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq L^{2}\sigma^{2}t+2L^{2}\sigma\varepsilon t+2L\sigma\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda s}w(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq L^{2}\sigma^{2}t+2L^{2}\sigma\varepsilon t+2L\sigma tW(t).\end{split}

Eventually, using the elementary inequalities,

\iint_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{0,t}}\lambda\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\quad\iint_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{0,t}}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}w(s,s)\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}s=\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}w(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

and

f(r,s)\leq 2L^{2}(\eta+s)+2Lw(s)
for
r<0
and
f(r,s)=0
for
r\geq 0
,
we get

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{0,t}}Z\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\leq 2L^{2}\sigma\varepsilon(\sigma+\varepsilon)+4L\lambda\sigma\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}w(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+2L^{2}\sigma\varepsilon t

\displaystyle\qquad+2\iint_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{0,\varepsilon\wedge t}}(L^{2}(\eta+s)+Lw(s))\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq 2L^{2}\sigma\varepsilon(\sigma+\varepsilon)+2L^{2}\varepsilon^{2}(\sigma+\varepsilon)+2L^{2}\sigma\varepsilon t+4L\lambda\sigma\varepsilon tW(t)+2L\varepsilon^{2}W(t\land\varepsilon).

We eventually get

(7.16)

\sum_{k=0}^{3}I_{k}\leq\varepsilon w^{2}(0)+2L^{2}\sigma^{2}t+4L^{2}\sigma\varepsilon t+2L^{2}\varepsilon(\sigma+\varepsilon)^{2}+4L\sigma(1+\lambda\varepsilon)tW(t)+2L\varepsilon^{2}W(t\land\varepsilon).

4. LHS and penalization

We want to use the first integral term in (
7.15
) to
derive a pointwise estimate for
w(t)
;

(
7.2
) and (
7.11
) yield

\displaystyle w(t)=w(t,t)\leq L(t-r)+w(r,t)\leq L(\tau+|t-r|)+w_{\tau}(r,t)

so that
we get for every
\vartheta,\vartheta_{\star}>1
conjugate coefficients

\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda t}w^{2}(t)\leq\frac{\vartheta}{\varepsilon}\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}e^{-2\lambda t}w_{\tau}^{2}(r,t)\,\mathrm{d}r+\vartheta_{\star}L^{2}(\tau+\varepsilon)^{2}\leq\frac{\vartheta}{\varepsilon}(I_{0}+I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3})+\vartheta_{\star}L^{2}(\tau+\varepsilon)^{2}.

(
7.16
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda t}w^{2}(t)\leq&(2\vartheta+\vartheta_{\star})L^{2}(\sigma+\varepsilon)^{2}+\vartheta\Big(w^{2}(0)+2L^{2}\sigma^{2}t/\varepsilon+4L^{2}\sigma t\Big)\\
&+\frac{4L(1+\lambda\varepsilon)\sigma\vartheta}{\varepsilon}tW(t)+2L\varepsilon\vartheta W(t\land\varepsilon).\end{split}

5. Conclusion.

Choosing
\varepsilon:=\sqrt{\sigma(\sigma\lor t)}
and assuming
\lambda\sqrt{T\sigma}\leq 1
,
we obtain

(7.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda t}w^{2}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\vartheta w^{2}(0)+(14\vartheta+4\vartheta_{\star})L^{2}\sigma(\sigma\lor t)+10\vartheta L\sqrt{\sigma(\sigma\lor t)}W(t).

Since the right hand side of (
7.17
) is an increasing
function of
t
,
(
7.17
) holds even if we substitute
the left hand side with

\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}w^{2}(s)
for every
s\in[0,t]
;
we thus obtain the inequality

W^{2}(t)\leq\vartheta w^{2}(0)+(14\vartheta+4\vartheta_{\star})L^{2}\sigma(\sigma\lor t)+10\vartheta L\sqrt{\sigma(\sigma\lor t)}W(t).

Using the elementary property for positive
a,b

(7.18)

W^{2}\leq a+2bW\quad\Rightarrow\quad W\leq b+\sqrt{b^{2}+a}\leq 2b+\sqrt{a},

we eventually obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}w(t)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\vartheta w^{2}(0)+(14\vartheta+4\vartheta_{\star})L^{2}\sigma(\sigma\lor t)\Big)^{1/2}+10\vartheta L\sqrt{\sigma(\sigma\lor t)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\vartheta}w(0)+C(\vartheta)L\sqrt{\sigma(\sigma\lor t)},\quad C(\vartheta):=(14\vartheta+4\vartheta_{\star})^{1/2}+10\vartheta.\qed

7.3.
Error estimates between discrete and EVI solutions

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
.
If
\mu\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})})
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
and
M_{\tau}\in\mathscr{M}(M^{0}_{\tau},\tau,T,L)
,
then for every
\vartheta>1

there exists a constant
C(\vartheta)
such that

W_{2}(\mu(t),M_{\tau}(t))\leq\Big(\sqrt{\vartheta}\,W_{2}(\mu_{0},M^{0}_{\tau})+C(\vartheta)L\sqrt{\tau(t+\tau)}\Big)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{+}t}\quad\text{for every }t\in[0,T].

Remark 7.6
.

When
\mu_{0}=M^{0}_{\tau}
and
\lambda\leq 0
we obtain the optimal error
estimate

W_{2}(\mu(t),M_{\tau}(t))\leq 13L\sqrt{\tau(t+\tau)}.

Proof.

We repeat the same argument of the previous proof, still assuming
\lambda>0
,
extending
M_{\tau},\bar{M}_{\tau},\bar{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}
as in (
7.10
)
and setting

w(r,s):=W_{2}(M_{\tau}(r),\mu(s)),\quad w_{\tau}(r,s):=W_{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau}(r),\mu(s)).

We use (

λ

-EVI
) for
\mu(s)
with
\nu=\bar{M}_{\tau}(r)
and
\Phi={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\tau}(r\lor 0)
and

(IEVI)
for
M_{\tau}(r)
with
\nu=\mu(s)
obtaining

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}}{2}W_{2}^{2}(M_{\tau}(r),\mu(s))

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}\Big(\tau|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(r)|_{2}^{2}+\left[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(r),\mu(s)\right]_{r}\Big)

\displaystyle s\in[0,T],r\in(-\infty,T)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu(s),\bar{M}_{\tau}(r))

\displaystyle\leq-\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}\left[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau}(r\lor 0),\mu(s)\right]_{r}

\displaystyle\text{in $\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(0,T)$, }r\in(-\infty,T).

Using
[
NS06
, Lemma 6.15]

we can sum the two contributions
obtaining

\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}w^{2}(r,s)+\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}w_{\tau}^{2}(r,s)\leq Z,\quad Z:=(2L^{2}\tau+2f(r,s))\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s},

where

f(r,s):=\begin{cases}LW_{2}(M_{\tau}(0),\mu(s))=Lw(0,s)&\text{if }r<0,\\
0&\text{if }r\geq 0.\end{cases}

Let
t\in[0,T]
and
\varepsilon>0
. Applying the Divergence Theorem in
Q_{0,t}^{\varepsilon}
(see Figure
1
) we get

(7.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}&\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda t}w_{\tau}^{2}(r,t)\,\mathrm{d}r\leq\int_{-\varepsilon}^{0}w_{\tau}^{2}(r,0)\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}\left(w_{\tau}^{2}(s,s)-w^{2}(s,s)\right)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}\left(w^{2}(s-\varepsilon,s)-w_{\tau}^{2}(s-\varepsilon,s)\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&+\iint_{Q_{0,t}^{\varepsilon}}Z\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Using

w(t,t)\leq w(r,t)+L(t-r)\leq w_{\tau}(r,t)+L(\tau+\varepsilon)\quad\text{if }t-\varepsilon\leq r\leq t,

we get for every
\vartheta,\vartheta_{\star}>1
conjugate coefficients (
\vartheta_{\star}=\vartheta/(\vartheta-1)
)

(7.20)

\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda t}w^{2}(t)\leq\frac{\vartheta}{\varepsilon}\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}e^{-2\lambda t}w_{\tau}^{2}(r,t)\,\mathrm{d}r+\vartheta_{\star}L^{2}(\tau+\varepsilon)^{2}.

Similarly to (
7.12
) we have

\displaystyle|w_{\tau}(s,s)-w(s,s)|\leq L\tau,\quad|w^{2}_{\tau}(s,s)-w^{2}(s,s)|\leq L\tau\Big(L\tau+2w(s)\Big)

and, after an integration,

(7.21)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda s}\left(w^{2}_{\tau}(s,s)-w^{2}(s,s)\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq{L^{2}t\tau^{2}}+2L\tau\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda s}w(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Performing the same computations for the third integral term at the RHS of (
7.19
) we end up with

(7.22)

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t}&e^{-2\lambda s}\left(w^{2}(s-\varepsilon,s)-w_{\tau}^{2}(s-\varepsilon,s)\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq{L^{2}t\tau^{2}}+2L\tau\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda s}w(s-\varepsilon,s)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq L^{2}t\tau(\tau+2\varepsilon)+2L\tau\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda s}w(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Finally, since if
r<0
we have
f(r,s)=Lw(0,s)\leq L^{2}s+Lw(s,s)
, then

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-1}\iint_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{0,t}}Z\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq 2L^{2}t\tau+\varepsilon^{-1}\iint_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{0,\varepsilon\land t}}2f(r,s)\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

(7.23)

\displaystyle\leq 2L^{2}t\tau+L^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+2L\varepsilon\sup_{0\leq s\leq\varepsilon\land t}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}w(s).

Using (
7.21
), (
7.22
), (
7.23
) in (
7.19
), we can rewrite the bound in (
7.20
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda t}w^{2}(t)&\leq\vartheta_{\star}L^{2}(\tau+\varepsilon)^{2}+\vartheta\Big(w^{2}(0)+2L^{2}t\tau^{2}/\varepsilon+2L^{2}t\tau+L^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+2L\varepsilon\sup_{0\leq s\leq\varepsilon\land t}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}w(s)\Big)\\
&\quad+\frac{4\vartheta L\tau}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda s}w(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Choosing
\varepsilon:=\sqrt{\tau(\tau\lor t)}

we get

\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda t}w^{2}(t)\leq 4\vartheta_{\star}L^{2}\tau(t\lor\tau)+\vartheta\Big(w^{2}(0)+5L^{2}\tau(t\lor\tau)\Big)+6\vartheta L\sqrt{\tau(t\lor\tau)}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}w(s).

A further application of
(
7.18
) yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}w(t)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\vartheta w^{2}(0)+(5\vartheta+4\vartheta_{\star})L^{2}\tau(t\lor\tau)\Big)^{1/2}+6\vartheta L\sqrt{\tau(t\lor\tau)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\vartheta}w(0)+C(\vartheta)L\sqrt{t+\tau}\sqrt{\tau},\quad C(\vartheta):=(5\vartheta+4\vartheta_{\star})^{1/2}+6\vartheta.\qed

As proved in the following, the limit curve of the interpolants

(M_{\tau})_{\tau>0}
of the Euler Scheme defined in
(
6.10
) is actually a
\lambda
-EVI solution of (
6.1
).

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
and let
n\mapsto\tau(n)
be a vanishing sequence of
time steps,
let
(\mu_{0,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
converging to

\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and
let
M_{n}\in\mathscr{M}(\mu_{0,n},\tau(n),T,L)
.
Then
M_{n}
is uniformly converging to a limit curve

\mu\in\mathrm{Lip}([0,T];\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})})
which
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
starting from
\mu_{0}
.

Proof.

Theorem
7.4

shows that
M_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence in

\mathrm{C}([0,T];\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})})
,
so that there exists a unique limit curve
\mu

as
n\to\infty
.
\mu
is also
L
-Lipschitz; moreover we observe that

(7.24)

W_{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau}(t),M_{\tau}(t))=W_{2}\left(M_{\tau}\left(\left\lfloor\frac{t}{\tau}\right\rfloor\tau\right),M_{\tau}(t)\right)\leq L\tau,\quad\text{ for any }t\in[0,T]

so that
\mu
is also the uniform limit of
\bar{M}_{\tau(n)}
.

Let us fix a reference measure
\nu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]
.

(IEVI)
and the
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
yield

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(M_{n}(t),\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\tau(n)|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau(n)}(t)|_{2}^{2}+\left[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\tau(n)},\nu\right]_{r}

\displaystyle\leq\tau(n)\,L^{2}+\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau(n)}(t),\nu)-\left[\Phi,\bar{M}_{\tau(n)}(t)\right]_{r}

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.
Integrating the above inequality in an interval
(t,t+h)\subset[0,T]

we get

(7.25)

\displaystyle\frac{W_{2}^{2}(M_{n}(t+h),\nu)-W_{2}^{2}(M_{n}(t),\nu)}{2h}

\displaystyle\leq\tau(n)L^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}\Big(\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau(n)}(s),\nu)-\left[\Phi,\bar{M}_{\tau(n)}(s)\right]_{r}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Notice that
as
n\to+\infty
, by (
7.24
), we have

\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\left[\Phi,\bar{M}_{\tau(n)}(s)\right]_{r}\geq\left[\Phi,\mu(s)\right]_{r}\quad\text{for every }s\in[0,T]

together with the uniform bound given by

\displaystyle\left|\left[\Phi,\bar{M}_{\tau(n)}(s)\right]_{r}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{M}_{\tau(n)}(s),\nu)+\frac{1}{2}|\Phi|_{2}^{2}\quad\text{for every }s\in[0,T].

Thanks to Fatou’s Lemma and the uniform convergence given by Theorem
7.4
,
we can pass to the limit as

n\to+\infty
in (
7.25
)
obtaining

\frac{W_{2}^{2}(\mu(t+h),\nu)-W_{2}^{2}(\mu(t),\nu)}{2h}\leq\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}\Big(\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu(s),\nu)-\left[\Phi,\mu(s)\right]_{r}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}s.

A further limit as
h\downarrow 0
yields

\frac{1}{2}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}W_{2}^{2}(\mu(t),\nu)\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu(t),\nu)-\left[\Phi,\mu(t)\right]_{r}

which provides (

λ

-EVI
).
∎

Appendix A
Comparison with
[
Pic19
]

In this section, we provide a brief comparison between the assumptions we required in order to develop a strong concept of solution to (
6.1
) and the hypotheses assumed in
[
Pic19
]
. We remind that the relation between our solution and the weaker notion studied in
[
Pic19
]
was exploited in Section
6.5
.
Here, we conclude with a further remark coming from the connections between our approximating scheme proposed in (
EE
) and the schemes proposed in
[
Cam+21
]
and
[
Pic19
]
.

We consider a finite time horizon
[0,T]
with
T>0
, the space

\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and we deal with measures in
\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and in
\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
i.e. compactly supported. We also deal with
single-valued

probability vector fields (PVF) for simplicity, which can be considered as everywhere defined
maps
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}:\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]=\nu
. This is indeed the framework examined in

[
Pic19
]
.

We start by recalling the assumptions required in
[
Pic19
]
for a PVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}:\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(H1)

there exists a constant
M>0
such that for all
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\sup_{(x,v)\in\supp({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu])}|v|\leq M\left(1+\sup_{x\in\supp(\nu)}|x|\right);

(H2)

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
satisfies the following
Lipschitz condition:
there exists a constant
L\geq 0
such that
for every
\Phi={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu],\ \Phi^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu^{\prime}]

there exists
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Lambda(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime})
satisfying

\int_{\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v_{0}-v_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1},v_{1})\leq L^{2}W_{2}^{2}(\nu,\nu^{\prime}),

with
\Lambda(\cdot,\cdot)
as in Definition
3.8
.

Remark A.1
.

We stress that actually in
[
Pic19
]

condition
(H2)
is local, meaning that
L
is allowed to
depend on
the radius
R
of a ball centered at
0
and containing
the supports of
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
. Thanks to assumption (H1),
it is easy to show that for every final time
T
all the discrete
solutions of the Explicit Euler scheme
and of the scheme of
[
Pic19
]

starting from an initial measure with support in
\mathrm{B}(0,R)

are supported in a ball
\mathrm{B}(0,R^{\prime})
where
R^{\prime}
solely depends
on

R
and
T
.
We can thus restrict the PVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
to the (geodesically convex) set of measures
with support in
\mathrm{B}(0,R^{\prime})

and act as
L
does not depend on the support of the measures.

Proposition A.2
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}:\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a
PVF

satisfying
(H2)
,
then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative for
\lambda=\frac{L^{2}+1}{2}
,
the Explicit Euler scheme is globally solvable in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, and

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
generates a
\lambda
-flow, whose trajectories are the limit
of the Explicit Euler scheme in each finite interval
[0,T]
.

Proof.

The
\lambda
-dissipativity comes from Lemma
4.7
. We prove that (
6.30
) holds.
Let
\nu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and take
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Lambda({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu],{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\delta_{0}])
such that

\int_{\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v^{\prime}-v^{\prime\prime}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\leq L^{2}W_{2}^{2}(\nu,\delta_{0})=L^{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\nu).

Since
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\delta_{0}]\in\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d})
by assumption, there exists
D>0
such that
\supp(\mathsf{v}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\delta_{0}])\subset B_{D}(0)
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle L^{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\nu)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v^{\prime}-v^{\prime\prime}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\geq\int_{\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d}}[|v^{\prime}|-D]_{+}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v^{\prime}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]-2D\int_{\mathsf{T}\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v^{\prime}|\,\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu],

where
[\,.\,]_{+}
denotes the positive part. By the trivial estimate
|v^{\prime}|\leq D+\frac{|v^{\prime}|^{2}}{4D}
, we conclude

|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\left(2D^{2}+L^{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\nu)\right).

Hence (
6.30
) and thus the global solvability of the
Explicit Euler scheme in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
by Proposition

6.20
. To conclude it is enough to apply Theorem

6.22
(a) and Theorem
7.7
.
∎

It is immediate to notice that the semi-discrete Lagrangian scheme
proposed in
[
Cam+21
]
coincides with the Explicit Euler
Scheme given in Definition
6.8
. In particular,
we can state the following
comparison between the limit obtained by the Explicit Euler scheme
(
EE
) (leading to the
\lambda
-EVI solution of
(
6.1
)) and that of the approximating LASs scheme proposed in

[
Pic19
]
(leading to a barycentric solution to
(
6.1
) in the sense of Definition
6.25
).

Corollary A.3
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
PVF
satisfying
(H1)
-
(H2)
,

\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let
T\in(0,+\infty)
. Let

(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence such that the LASs scheme

(\mu^{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of
[
Pic19
, Definition 3.1]

converges uniformly-in-time and let
(M_{\tau_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the
affine interpolants of the Explicit Euler Scheme defined in (
6.10
), with
\tau_{k}=\frac{T}{n_{k}}
. Then
(\mu^{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(M_{\tau_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converge to the same limit curve
\mu\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
which is the unique
\lambda
-
EVI
solution of (
6.1
) in
[0,T]
.

Proof.

By Proposition
A.2
,
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a

\left(\frac{L^{2}+1}{2}\right)
-dissipative MPVF s.t.
\mathrm{\mathcal{}}{M}(\mu_{0},\tau,T,\tilde{L})\neq\emptyset
for every
\tau>0
,
where
\tilde{L}>0
is a suitable constant depending on
\mu_{0}
and

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
. Thus by Theorem
7.7
,
(M_{\tau_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
uniformly converges to a
\lambda
-EVI solution
\mu\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
which is unique since
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
generates a

\left(\frac{L^{2}+1}{2}\right)
-flow.
Since we start from a compactly supported
\mu_{0}
, the semi-discrete
Lagrangian scheme of
[
Cam+21
]
and our Euler Scheme
actually coincide. To conclude we apply
[
Cam+21
, Theorem 4.1]
obtaining that
\mu
is also the limit of the LASs scheme.
∎

We conclude that among the possibly not-unique (see
[
Cam+21
]
) barycentric solutions to (
6.1
) - i.e. the solutions in the sense of
[
Pic19
]
/Definition
6.25
- we are
selecting
only one (the
\lambda
-EVI solution), which turns out to be the one associated with the LASs approximating scheme.

In light of this observation, we revisit an interesting example studied in
[
Pic19
, Section 7.1]
and
[
Cam+21
, Section 6]
.

Example A.4

(Splitting particle)
.

For every
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
define:

B(\nu):=\sup\left\{x:\nu(]-\infty,x])\leq\frac{1}{2}\right\},\qquad\eta(\nu):=\nu(]-\infty,B(\nu)])-\frac{1}{2},

so that
\nu(\{B(\nu)\})=\eta(\nu)+\frac{1}{2}-\nu(]-\infty,B(\nu)[)
.
We define the PVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]:=\int{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{x}[\nu]\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)
, by

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{x}[\nu]:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{-1}&\textrm{if}\ x<B(\nu)\\
\delta_{1}&\textrm{if}\ x>B(\nu)\\
\frac{1}{\nu(\{B(\nu)\})}\left(\eta\delta_{1}+\left(\frac{1}{2}-\nu(]-\infty,B(\nu)[)\right)\delta_{-1}\right)&\textrm{if}\ x=B(\nu),\nu(\{B(\nu)\})>0.\end{array}\right.

By
[
Pic19
, Proposition 7.2]
,
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
satisfies assumptions
(H1)
-
(H2)
with
L=0
and the LASs scheme admits a unique limit. Moreover, the solution
\mu:[0,T]\to\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
obtained as limit of LASs, is given by

(A.1)

\begin{split}\mu_{t}(A)=&\mu_{0}((A\cap]-\infty,B(\mu_{0})-t[)+t)+\mu_{0}((A\cap]B(\mu_{0})+t,+\infty[)-t)\\
&+\frac{1}{\mu_{0}(\{B(\mu_{0})\})}\left(\eta\delta_{B(\mu_{0})+t}(A)+(\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}(]-\infty,B(\mu_{0})[))\delta_{B(\mu_{0})-t}(A)\right).\end{split}

By Corollary
A.3
, (
A.1
) is the (unique)
\lambda
-EVI solution of (
6.1
).
In particular:

i)

if
\mu_{0}=\frac{1}{b-a}\mathcal{L}\llcorner_{[a,b]}
, i.e. the normalized Lebesgue measure restricted to
[a,b]
, we get
\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{b-a}\mathcal{L}\llcorner_{[a-t,\frac{a+b}{2}-t]}+\frac{1}{b-a}\mathcal{L}\llcorner_{[\frac{a+b}{2}+t,b+t]}
;

ii)

if
\mu_{0}=\delta_{x_{0}}
, we get
\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{0}+t}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{0}-t}
.

Notice that, in case (i), since
\mu_{t}\ll\mathcal{L}
for all
t\in(0,T)
, i.e.
\mu_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R})
, we can also apply Theorem
6.30
to conclude that
\mu
is the
\lambda
-EVI solution of (
6.1
) with
\mu_{0}=\frac{1}{b-a}\mathcal{L}\llcorner_{[a,b]}
. Moreover, take
\varepsilon>0
, and consider case (i) where we denote by
\mu^{\varepsilon}_{0}
the initial datum and by
\mu^{\varepsilon}
the corresponding
\lambda
-EVI solution to (
6.1
) with
a=x_{0}-\varepsilon
,
b=x_{0}+\varepsilon
. We can apply (
6.31
) with
\mu_{0}=\mu_{0}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mu_{1}=\delta_{x_{0}}
in order to give another proof that, for all
t\in[0,T]
, the
W_{2}
-limit of
S_{t}[\mu_{0}^{\varepsilon}]
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, that is
S_{t}[\delta_{x_{0}}]=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{0}+t}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{0}-t}
, is a
\lambda
-EVI solution starting from
\delta_{x_{0}}
. Thus we end up with (ii).

Dealing with case (ii), we recall that, if
\mu_{0}=\delta_{x_{0}}
then
also the stationary curve
\bar{\mu}_{t}=\delta_{x_{0}}
, for all

t\in[0,T]
, satisfies the barycentric property of Definition

6.25
(see
[
Cam+21
, Example 6.1]
), thus it is a
solution in the sense of
[
Pic19
]
.
However,
\bar{\mu}
is not a
\lambda
-EVI solution since it does not coincide with the curve given by ii).
This fact can also be checked by a direct calculation as follows:
we find
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
such that

(A.2)

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{t},\nu)>\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{t},\nu)-\left[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu],\bar{\mu}_{t}\right]_{r}\qquad t\in(0,T),

where
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
is the dissipativity constant of the PVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
coming from the proof of Proposition
A.2
. Notice
that the LHS of (
A.2
) is always zero since

t\mapsto\bar{\mu}_{t}=\delta_{0}
is constant. Take
\nu=\mathcal{L}\llcorner_{[0,1]}
so that we get
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]=\int{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{x}[\nu]\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)
, with
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{x}[\nu]=\delta_{1}
if
x>\frac{1}{2}
,
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{x}[\nu]=\delta_{-1}
if
x<\frac{1}{2}
. Noting that
\Lambda({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu],\delta_{0})=\{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]\otimes\delta_{0}\}
, by using the characterization in Theorem
3.9
we compute

\left[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu],\delta_{0}\right]_{r}=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle x,v\rangle\,\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]=\int_{0}^{1/2}\langle x,v\rangle\,\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{x}[\nu](v)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{1/2}^{1}\langle x,v\rangle\,\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{x}[\nu](v)\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{4}.

Since
W_{2}^{2}(\delta_{0},\nu)=\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\nu)=\frac{1}{3}
, we have

\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{t},\nu)-\left[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu],\bar{\mu}_{t}\right]_{r}=\frac{1}{6}-\frac{1}{4}<0,

and thus we obtain the desired inequality (
A.2
) with
\nu=\mathcal{L}\llcorner_{[0,1]}
.

Appendix B
Wasserstein differentiability along curves

In general, if
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
is a locally absolutely continuous curve and
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, then the map
[0,+\infty)\ni s\mapsto W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s},\nu)
is locally absolutely continuous and thus differentiable in a set of full measure
A({\mu,\nu})\subset(0,+\infty)
which, in principle, depends both on
\mu
and
\nu
. What Theorem
3.11
shows is that, independently of
\nu
, there is a full measure set
A(\mu)
, depending only on
\mu
, where this map is left and right differentiable. If moreover
\nu
and
t\in A(\mu)
are such that there is a unique optimal transport plan between them, we can actually conclude that such a map is differentiable at
t
.

We want to highlight how this result is optimal giving an example of a locally absolutely continuous curve
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
s.t. the full measure set of differentiability points of the map
[0,+\infty)\ni s\mapsto W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s},\nu)
depends also on
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. To do that it is enough to show that

\text{ for every }t_{0}\in A(\mu)\text{ there exist }\nu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\text{ and }\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t_{0}},\nu_{0})\text{ s.t. }L(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1})\neq L(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}),

where
A(\mu)
is as in Theorem
2.10
and, for
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
s.t.
\mathsf{x}^{0}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\mu_{t}
, we define

L(\boldsymbol{\gamma}):=\int_{\mathsf{X}^{2}}\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x),x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,y).

Indeed this will imply that
\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t_{0}})_{\sharp}\mu_{t_{0}},\nu_{0}\right]_{r}\neq\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t_{0}})_{\sharp}\mu_{t_{0}},\nu_{0}\right]_{l}
, hence the non differentiability at
t_{0}
.

Let us consider two regular functions
u:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
r:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
s.t.
|u_{t}|=1
for every
t\geq 0
. Let
\omega:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be defined as the orthogonal direction to
u_{t}
:

\omega_{t}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}u_{t},\quad\quad t\geq 0.

Being the norm of
u
constant in time, there exists some regular
\lambda:(0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
s.t.
\dot{u}_{t}=\lambda_{t}\omega_{t}
for every
t>0
. Finally we define

\displaystyle x_{1}:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle\qquad x_{1}(t):=r_{t}u_{t},

\displaystyle x_{2}:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle\qquad x_{2}(t):=-r_{t}u_{t},

\displaystyle\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle\qquad\mu_{t}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{x_{1}(t)}+\delta_{x_{2}(t)}\right).

Observe that
\dot{x}_{1}(t)=\dot{r}_{t}u_{t}+r_{t}\dot{u}_{t}=-\dot{x}_{2}(t)
for every
t>0
. Moreover, for every
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\left(\frac{1}{2}\varphi(x_{1}(t))+\frac{1}{2}\varphi(x_{2}(t))\right)=\frac{1}{2}\nabla\varphi(x_{1}(t))\,\dot{x}_{1}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\varphi(x_{2}(t))\,\dot{x}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle v_{t}(x),\nabla\varphi(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t},

where

\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x):=\begin{cases}\dot{x}_{1}(t)\quad&\text{ if }x=x_{1}(t),\\
\dot{x}_{2}(t)\quad&\text{ if }x=x_{2}(t),\end{cases}\quad t>0.

Hence, the above defined vector field
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}
solves the continuity equation with
\mu_{t}
. Let
t_{0}\in A(\mu)
and let us define
\omega_{0}:=\omega(t_{0})
,
\nu_{0}:=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{-\omega_{0}}
and the plans
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t_{0}},\nu_{0})
by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}:=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{1}(t_{0})}\otimes\delta_{\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{2}(t_{0})}\otimes\delta_{-\omega_{0}},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}:=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{2}(t_{0})}\otimes\delta_{\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{1}(t_{0})}\otimes\delta_{-\omega_{0}}.

Notice that they are optimal since any plan in
\Gamma(\mu_{t_{0}},\nu_{0})
has the same cost, being the points
\omega_{0},x_{1}(t_{0}),x_{2}(t_{0}),-\omega_{0}
the vertexes of a rhombus. Finally, we compute
L(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1})
and
L(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2})
:

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle x-y,\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\langle\dot{x}_{1}(t_{0}),x_{1}(t_{0})-\omega_{0}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\dot{x}_{2}(t_{0}),x_{2}(t_{0})+\omega_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\dot{x}_{1}(t_{0}),x_{1}(t_{0})-\omega_{0}\rangle=\langle\dot{r}_{t_{0}}u_{t_{0}}+r_{t_{0}}\dot{u}_{t_{0}},r_{t_{0}}u_{t_{0}}-\omega_{0}\rangle=r_{t_{0}}\dot{r}_{t_{0}}-r_{t_{0}}\lambda_{t_{0}},

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle x-y,\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\langle\dot{x}_{2}(t_{0}),x_{2}(t_{0})-\omega_{0}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\dot{x}_{1}(t_{0}),x_{1}(t_{0})+\omega_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\dot{x}_{1}(t_{0}),x_{1}(t_{0})+\omega_{0}\rangle=\langle\dot{r}_{t_{0}}u_{t_{0}}+r_{t_{0}}\dot{u}_{t_{0}},r_{t_{0}}u_{t_{0}}+\omega_{0}\rangle=r_{t_{0}}\dot{r}_{t_{0}}+r_{t_{0}}\lambda_{t_{0}}.

In this way, if
r_{t_{0}}\neq 0
and
\lambda_{t_{0}}\neq 0
we have
L(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1})\neq L(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2})
. A possible choice for
u
and
r
satisfying the assumptions is

u_{t}:=(\cos(t),\sin(t)),\quad\quad r_{t}=1,\quad\quad t\geq 0,

so that
\lambda_{t}=1
for every
t>0
.

Appendix C
Support function and Dini derivatives

We recall the following characterization of the
closed convex hull
\overline{\operatorname{co}}(C)
of a set
C
(i.e. the intersection of all the closed convex sets containing
C
)
in a Banach space.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
Z
be a Banach space and let
C\subset Z
be nonempty. Then
v\in\overline{\operatorname{co}}(C)
if and only if

(C.1)

\langle z^{*},v\rangle\leq\sup_{c\in C}\,\langle z^{*},c\rangle\quad\forall\,z^{*}\in Z^{*}.

Moreover if
C
is bounded, it is enough to have (
C.1
) holding for every
z^{*}\in W
, with
W
a dense subset of
Z^{*}
.

Proof.

The result is a direct consequence of Hahn-Banach theorem.

Concerning the last assertion, observe that the function

Z^{*}\ni z^{*}\mapsto\sup_{c\in C}\,\langle z^{*},c\rangle

is Lipschitz continuous if
C
is bounded. Hence, if (
C.1
) holds only for some
W\subset Z^{*}
dense, then it holds for the whole
Z^{*}
.
∎

Let us state and prove a simple lemma that allows us to pass from a differential inequality for the right upper Dini derivative to the corresponding distributional inequality (see also
[
MS20
, Lemma A.1]
and
[
Gál57
]
).

Lemma C.2
.

Let
(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}
be an open interval (bounded or unbounded) and
let
\zeta,\eta:(a,b)\to\mathbb{R}
be s.t.
\zeta
is continuous in

(a,b)

and
\eta
is measurable
and locally bounded from above in
(a,b)
. If

{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}\zeta(t)\leq\eta(t)\quad\text{ for every }t\in(a,b),

then the above inequality holds also in the sense of distributions, meaning that

-\int_{a}^{b}\zeta(t)\varphi^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{a}^{b}\eta(t)\varphi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{ for every }\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{c}(a,b).

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{c}(a,b)
, then there exist
a<x<y<b
s.t. the support of
\varphi
is contained in
[x,y]
; since
\eta
is locally bounded from above, there exists a positive constant
C>0
s.t.
\eta(t)\leq C
for every
t\in[x,y]
. Then the function
t\mapsto\zeta(t)-Ct
is s.t.

{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}(\zeta(t)-Ct)\leq 0\quad\text{ for every }t\in[x,y]

so that it is decreasing in
[x,y]
and hence a function of bounded variation in
[x,y]
. Its distributional derivative is hence a non positive measure
T
on
[x,y]
whose absolutely continuous part (w.r.t. the
1
-dimensional Lebesgue measure on
[x,y]
) coincides a.e. with the right upper Dini derivative. Then we have

-\int_{a}^{b}(\zeta(t)-Ct)\varphi^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=T(\varphi)=\int_{a}^{b}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}^{\kern-3.0pt+}(\zeta(t)-Ct)\varphi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+T_{s}(\varphi)\leq\int_{a}^{b}(\eta-C)\varphi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
T_{s}
is the singular part of
T
. This immediately gives the thesis.
∎
